# OT1997 chunk 13 assignment evidence

Exact public event and opinion-topology sections copied from current-term Records. Prior counts are not a source for the census.

## 1997-11-04 ? Bates v. United States, No. 96-7185; Seventh Circuit No. 95-2203.

Source: bates_v_united_states_merits_1997-11-04.md

### Event

Bates v. United States, No. 96-7185. Merits decision, November 4, 1997; argued October 7, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. On certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), the Court reviews the Seventh Circuit's reinstatement of a twelve-count indictment, 96 F.3d 964, after pretrial dismissal. The question is whether knowing and willful misapplication under the version of 20 U.S.C. §1097(a) applicable to the alleged 1990 conduct additionally requires an intent to injure or defraud.

### Opinion Topology

Justice Ginsburg delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

| Writing | Author | Joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer, in full | Nine-Justice controlling opinion; one signed lead plenary merits opinion counted for Ginsburg |

## 1997-11-04 ? State Oil Co. v. Khan, No. 96-871.

Source: state_oil_co_v_khan_merits_1997-11-04.md

### Event

State Oil Co. v. Khan, No. 96-871. Merits decision, November 4, 1997; argued October 7, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. On certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), the Court reviews the Seventh Circuit's antitrust disposition, 93 F.3d 1358. The question is whether a vertical agreement setting a maximum resale price is unlawful per se under Sherman Act §1 and whether Albrecht should remain controlling.

### Opinion Topology

Justice Scalia delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

All nine support vacatur of the antitrust component and remand under the rule of reason, leaving the contract affirmance untouched. The case requires one signed plenary opinion openly reconsidering the controlling per se rule and explaining the preserved boundaries. That form and scope precede author selection.
The actual earlier opinion tables were reread before assignment: Brown is excluded; Bates counts once for Ginsburg. **Counts:** Stone-Zsela 0; Stevens 0; O'Connor 0; Scalia 0; Kennedy 0; Souter 0; Thomas 0; Ginsburg 1; Breyer 0. **Last counted event:** Bates, November 4, 1997, first in the same-day caption order. **Eligible authors:** all nine fully join the common classification, reason for overruling, continuing exposure and component remedy. **Minimum:** 0. **Balance pool:** all nine. Stone assigns to **Scalia** for fit: his Business Electronics analysis of actual price agreements and competitive classification, together with his Hubbard requirement of a concrete reason for statutory correction, directly suits explaining why this admitted agreement receives a different legal category without a fictitious unilateral-policy exception. O'Connor's precedent-stability analysis and Stone's approved candid-overruling rationale are strong alternatives; Scalia's existing work at the precise agreement/classification boundary supplies the concrete advantage. No necessary accommodation remains unresolved and no authorship-based vote change is modeled.
| Writing | Author | Joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, in full | Nine-Justice controlling opinion; one signed lead plenary merits opinion counted for Scalia |

## 1997-11-24 ? United States v. Rybar; Third Circuit No. 95-3185; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

Source: united_states_v_rybar_merits_1997-11-24.md

### Event

United States v. Rybar, Third Circuit No. 95-3185. Merits decision, November 24, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. The Court reviews under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) the December 30, 1996 judgment, 103 F.3d 273, sustaining possession convictions under 18 U.S.C. §922(o). Conditional pleas preserve the Commerce Clause and Second Amendment challenges to Counts I and III.

### Opinion Topology

Kennedy delivers the opinion of the Court. All other Justices join Parts I–II on commerce power and personal invocation. Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer join Part III's vacatur and remand. Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I–II and dissents from Part III and the judgment.

### Assignment statements

The actual earlier tables were reread. **Counts before each assignment:** Stone-Zsela 0; Stevens 0; O'Connor 0; Scalia 1; Kennedy 0; Souter 1; Thomas 0; Ginsburg 1; Breyer 0. **Last counted event:** Jeffries, with Souter's one lead counted once despite its parts; Brown and Monroe are excluded.
For the separately resolved commerce component, Stone assigns Part I. **Eligible authors and minimum-plus-one pool:** all nine fully support that component's propositions; minimum zero. He selects **Kennedy** for fit: his American Life League and Pic-A-State opinions, applying the Lopez opinion of the Court that he joined, and his Hale join directly equip him to explain the enacted anti-evasion market mechanism while keeping commerce authority separate from the personal-right inquiry. O'Connor's Love/Hale authorship offers a strong alternative; Kennedy's specific coordination with the remote-effects boundary supplies the advantage. Stone's Lopez authorship and approved commerce analysis are compatible, but a Hale application adding no Commerce doctrine supplies no superior reason for self-assignment.
The eight-member arms vacatur/remand coalition excludes Stone; **Stevens**, its senior Justice, assigns Parts II–III to **Kennedy**, who fully supports the personal-invocation analysis and bounded remedy and can carry the entire opinion consistently. Stone's join in Part II is a rationale join, not a separate judgment disposition authorizing him to assign that part. All eight Associates are full-join eligible for Parts II–III; the Chief's balance rule does not bind these Associate assignments. The same actual counts are recorded. One eligible author carries every part, so the opinion is not split among authors or counted more than once.
| Writing | Author | Exact joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–II | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Nine-Justice commerce and personal-invocation holdings |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III | Kennedy | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Eight-Justice vacatur and remand |
| Opinion concurring in Parts I–II and dissenting from Part III and the judgment | Stone-Zsela | None | Independent as-applied affirmance; noncontrolling |
Kennedy receives one signed lead plenary merits count. No other writing counts. The final holdings and material separate reasoning are set out completely in the Public Projection and also constitute this Record's internal law; no Marks question arises.

## 1997-12-02 ? Salinas v. United States, No. 96-738.

Source: salinas_v_united_states_merits_1997-12-02.md

### Event

Salinas v. United States, No. 96-738. Merits decision, December 2, 1997; argued October 8, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. On certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), the Court reviews affirmance of Salinas's two federal bribery convictions and RICO conspiracy conviction. The questions concern a demonstrable effect on federal funds under §666 and personal commission or agreement to commit two predicates under §1962(d).

### Opinion Topology

Justice Thomas delivers the opinion of the Court on both questions, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

All nine affirm on both questions and fully join S1R, S2R and S3 with every express reservation. Two statutory questions warrant one signed plenary opinion with distinct holding blocks, not two counted opinions. No separate writing is needed.
| Writing | Author | Joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer, in full | Nine-Justice controlling opinion on both questions; one signed lead plenary merits opinion counted for Thomas |

## 1997-12-08 ? Richenberg v. Cohen; review of Richenberg v. Perry, Eighth Circuit No. 95-4181. No separate Supreme Court docket is supplied.

Source: richenberg_v_cohen_merits_1997-12-08.md

### Event

Richenberg v. Cohen; review of Richenberg v. Perry, Eighth Circuit No. 95-4181, 97 F.3d 256. Merits decision, December 8, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. On certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), the Court reviews affirmance of summary judgment sustaining an honorable military discharge. The question concerns genuine individualized rebuttal under the identity-statement policy. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established here.

### Opinion Topology

Justice O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer. Justice Stevens dissents, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Souter and Ginsburg. The Chief Justice also files an additional dissenting statement specifying the conditions for further military action under his proposed remedy.

### Assignment statements

The constitutional application warrants one signed plenary opinion applying an established standard to the disputed administrative record, with a dissent explaining the opposite application. This form and the propositions were fixed before counts or authors. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer affirm and fully join the common rationale. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg would reverse and require this discharge to be set aside. Their shared ground is the as-applied equality defect; no independent speech, APA or due-process ground is needed. Stone's additional remedy precision is published separately without attributing it as new text adopted by the Associates.
| Writing | Author | Joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer, in full | Five-Justice controlling constitutional application; one signed lead plenary merits opinion counted for O'Connor |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg, in full | Common as-applied equality objection and discharge-removal remedy; noncontrolling |
| Additional dissenting statement | Stone-Zsela | None | Further-action remedy precision accompanying his full join to Stevens's dissent; noncontrolling and noncounted |

## 1997-12-09 ? Trest v. Cain, No. 96-7901; review of Trest v. Whitley, Fifth Circuit No. 94-40515.

Source: trest_v_cain_merits_1997-12-09.md

### Event

Trest v. Cain, No. 96-7901. Merits decision, December 9, 1997; argued November 10, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. On certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), the Court reviews the Fifth Circuit's denial of federal habeas relief and considers whether an appellate court must raise state procedural default when the State has not relied on it. Trest challenges prior Mississippi guilty pleas used to enhance his Louisiana sentence; the merits of that challenge are not decided.

### Opinion Topology

Justice Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

This case warrants one signed plenary merits opinion deciding the habeas procedural-default question and giving a bounded mandate. All nine directly join the same no-duty rule, rejection of the lower compulsory premise, reservations and vacatur/remand. There is no distinct substantive separate position or unresolved conditional join. Form and propositions were fixed before counts and author selection. This is a counted signed lead merits decision after argument even though its substantive subject is a procedural defense; it is not an independent filing or jurisdictional action.
| Writing | Author | Joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, in full | Nine-Justice no-duty holding and bounded mandate; one signed lead plenary merits opinion counted for Stevens |

## 1997-12-10 ? Hudson v. United States, No. 96-976.

Source: hudson_v_united_states_merits_1997-12-10.md

### Event

Hudson v. United States, No. 96-976. Argued October 8, 1997; decided December 10, 1997. Certiorari to the Tenth Circuit, 92 F.3d 1026, reviewing reinstatement of a criminal prosecution after civil banking sanctions.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I and III: classification framework, combined application, and disposition | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II: Halper's personal-penalty application | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg |
| Concurrence in Parts I and III and the judgment | Stone-Zsela | None |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas |

### Assignment statements

The argued merits question requires one signed opinion applying the distinct classification and retained personal-penalty inquiries. Part I states the civil-intent and actual-operation framework; Part II applies Halper to the monetary assessments; Part III evaluates the qualified banking restriction together with the financial sanctions, affirms, and reserves identity. These substantive parts and the separate grounds are fixed before authors and counts.
The actual lead-opinion tables in the [entering extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK2_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md) were recounted from scratch: Bates and Foster—Ginsburg; Khan—Scalia; Jeffries—Souter once; Rybar—Kennedy once; Parker and Richenberg—O'Connor; Salinas—Thomas; Trest—Stevens. Brown, Monroe, and Jefferson are excluded procedural or summary actions. Counts before assignment: Stone-Zsela 0; Stevens 1; O'Connor 2; Scalia 1; Kennedy 1; Souter 1; Thomas 1; Ginsburg 2; Breyer 0. Last counted event: Trest, December 9, 1997. The Chief belongs to the unanimous judgment coalition and assigns. Whole-opinion eligible authors: Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Minimum 0; pool: Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer. Stone's partial join and Scalia/Thomas's judgment-only agreement do not confer full-opinion eligibility. Breyer is selected for fit: his Hendricks opinion directly articulates meaningful cumulative-operation examination under clearest proof, and his Ursery join preserves Halper's distinct field. Stevens offers a strong alternative on personal sanctions, but Breyer's recent coordination of the framework and actual application provides the closer fit and lower count. One eligible author carries every part; no divided authorship or expansion accommodation is created.
| Writing | Author | Exact joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I and III | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Seven direct votes for classification framework, combined application and remedy |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Six direct votes for retained Halper accounting application |
| Concurrence in Parts I and III and the judgment | Stone-Zsela | None | Approved classification ground; no join in the independent accounting ground |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Successive-criminal-prosecution account; noncontrolling |
Breyer receives one signed lead count. No Marks aggregation supplies authority; all controlling propositions have direct majorities. The complete holdings, precedent treatment, resulting law and remedy in the Public Projection are also this Record's internal law.

## 1997-12-15 ? Bay Area Laundry and Dry Cleaning Pension Trust Fund v. Ferbar Corp. of California, No. 96-370.

Source: bay_area_laundry_v_ferbar_merits_1997-12-15.md

### Event

Bay Area Laundry and Dry Cleaning Pension Trust Fund v. Ferbar Corp. of California, No. 96-370. Argued November 10, 1997; decided December 15, 1997. Certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, 73 F.3d 971, concerning limitations on withdrawal-liability collection.

### Opinion Topology

Justice Breyer delivers the opinion of the Court. All eight other Justices join Parts I–II on accrual, installments, acceleration and the bounded remand. Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg join Part III on the first-installment exclusion; Stone-Zsela agrees in that component's judgment only. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

One signed argued merits opinion is warranted. Parts I–II decide accrual, independent installments, acceleration and the bounded remand; Part III supplies the distinct case-specific first-claim exclusion. These propositions and form are fixed before counts. Actual prior tables count Bates/Foster—Ginsburg; Khan—Scalia; Jeffries—Souter; Rybar—Kennedy; Parker/Richenberg—O'Connor; Salinas—Thomas; Trest—Stevens; Hudson—Breyer. Each multipart lead counts once. Brown, Monroe and Jefferson do not count. Counts before assignment: Stone-Zsela 0; Stevens 1; O'Connor 2; Scalia 1; Kennedy 1; Souter 1; Thomas 1; Ginsburg 2; Breyer 1. Last counted event: Hudson, December 10.
The Chief assigns from the unanimous reversal coalition. Eligible whole-opinion authors: all eight Associates; Stone's fallback/partial relationship does not qualify. Minimum 1; pool all eight, each no more than one above minimum. Breyer is selected for the direct fit of his Milwaukee opinion, which precisely distinguishes actual payment, statutory default and optional acceleration from the computational start. Ginsburg's Inter-Modal treatment of substantive rights and enforcement is a strong alternative, but Breyer's actual work on this statutory sequence is closer and his count is lower. No authorship entitlement or expansion bargain is used. One author fully supports all parts; there is no split assignment.
| Writing | Author | Exact joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–II | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Nine direct votes on accrual, installments, acceleration and bounded remand |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Eight direct votes on petition-representation exclusion of first installment; Stone agrees in that judgment only |
Breyer counts once. No separate writing and no Marks issue. The Public Projection states the complete internal controlling law, remedy and reservations without an additional unprojected holding.

## 1997-12-22 ? Tanford v. Brand; Seventh Circuit No. 96-3053, 104 F.3d 982. No separate Supreme Court docket is supplied.

Source: tanford_v_brand_merits_1997-12-22.md

### Event

Tanford v. Brand, review of the Seventh Circuit's judgment in No. 96-3053, 104 F.3d 982. Decided December 22, 1997, on the merits of the challenge to Indiana University's official commencement invocations and benedictions. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I: live controversy and official attribution | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts II–III: constitutional application and relief | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Dissent, Part I: adult-setting ground | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas |
| Dissent, Part II: historical accommodation and compulsion account | Scalia | Thomas |
| Separate dissenting statement | Kennedy | None |

### Assignment statements

Prior signed lead opinions: Bates/Foster—Ginsburg; Khan—Scalia; Jeffries—Souter; Rybar/Joiner—Kennedy; Parker/Richenberg—O'Connor; Salinas/Chicago—Thomas; Trest—Stevens; Hudson/Bay Area—Breyer. Brown, Monroe and Jefferson are excluded; multipart leads count once. Counts before assignment: Stone-Zsela 0; Stevens 1; O'Connor 2; Scalia 1; Kennedy 2; Souter 1; Thomas 2; Ginsburg 2; Breyer 2. Last counted event: Joiner, December 15.
The Chief assigns from the six-Justice reversing coalition. Eligible whole-opinion authors: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Minimum 0; pool Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter. Stone assigns to himself for the concrete combination of fit and precision in this adopted development: his Pinette access opinion preserves genuinely private religious expression, while his Tanford formulation directly identifies official selection and conduct of devotional exercises as the operative act independently of adult coercion. Souter's Lee concurrence and Good News contextual opinion make him a strong alternative. Both can carry the adopted boundary; Stone's exact current formulation supplies that boundary within Lemon as Agostini modified it, and his lower count favors this assignment within the pool. No new accommodation or inference of colleague agreement supplies the choice. One fully eligible author carries all parts.

## 1998-01-21 ? Lunding v. New York Tax Appeals Tribunal, No. 96-1462.

Source: lunding_v_new_york_tax_appeals_tribunal_merits_1998-01-21.md

### Event

Lunding v. New York Tax Appeals Tribunal, No. 96-1462. Plenary merits decision, January 21, 1998; argued November 5, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the New York Court of Appeals, 89 N.Y.2d 283, 675 N.E.2d 816 (1996), which sustained N.Y. Tax Law § 631(b)(6). The question is whether denying qualifying alimony deductions in calculating nonresidents' New York-source income, within the complete nonresident tax formula, violates Article IV's Privileges and Immunities Clause. Equal Protection and Commerce Clause claims are outside this review.

Christopher and Barbara Lunding were Connecticut residents. Christopher earned income practicing law in New York and paid alimony to a Connecticut former spouse under a Connecticut decree. They claimed a proportionate deduction using approximately 48% of his business income as attributable to New York. Disallowance produced an additional assessment of $3,724 plus interest.

### Opinion Topology

Thomas delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer. The same seven Justices adopt both holdings and all their qualifications. Ginsburg dissents, joined in full by Kennedy, on both the income-attribution and independent home-State personal-expense grounds.

### Assignment statements

The actual opinion tables in the [twenty-four-event extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md), [Lee](lee_v_harcleroad_threshold_1998-01-20.md), [Buchanan](buchanan_v_angelone_merits_1998-01-21.md) and [LaChance/McManus](lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md) were recounted from scratch. The counted authors are Ginsburg—Bates/Foster/Clanton; Scalia—Khan/Smith; Souter—Jeffries/Valot/LaChance; Kennedy—Rybar/Joiner/Rouse; O'Connor—Parker/Richenberg/Buchanan; Thomas—Salinas/Chicago; Stevens—Trest/Baker; Breyer—Hudson/Bay Area; Stone-Zsela—Tanford/Fink. Each multipart lead counts once. Brown's filing order, Monroe's summary disposition, Jefferson's jurisdictional dismissal, Rogers's DIG and Lee's threshold decision do not count. An independent read-only recount of the original twenty-four tables and a direct mechanical recount including both actual January 21 tables confirm twenty-two counted leads; no reported running tally supplies an author.
**Counts before assignment:** Stone-Zsela 2; Stevens 2; O'Connor 3; Scalia 2; Kennedy 3; Souter 3; Thomas 2; Ginsburg 3; Breyer 2. **Last counted event:** LaChance v. Erickson / McManus, January 21, 1998, following Buchanan and preceding Lunding alphabetically. Its actual opinion table supplies Souter's single lead; neither peer supplies substantive law.
The Chief assigns from the seven-Justice reversal coalition. **Eligible authors:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer; each supports every governing proposition, qualification and remedial condition in full. Kennedy and Ginsburg are outside the judgment coalition. The eligible minimum is 2, and all seven eligible authors have no more than 3, so **the balance pool is the entire eligible set**.
The Chief selects **Thomas** for the concrete work of examining the actual tax computation, separating a legitimate source-allocation distinction from an unsupported offset, and stating constitutional invalidity without prescribing a monetary remedy. His own Fulton and Oregon Waste opinions demonstrate those tasks, including explicit limits on the constitutional field and on relief. Prior authorship creates no entitlement; the present fit is the coordination of those tasks under Article IV's distinct inquiry. O'Connor's Reich opinion supplies the strongest alternative for the postpayment-remedy component, but Thomas combines the computation/justification work with Fulton's conditional correction and has the lower count. Stone's approved position supports the entire opinion and makes him eligible at count 2; it supplies no formulation that Thomas cannot express equally faithfully, and Thomas's demonstrated combined tax-operation and remedial work gives the case-specific fit advantage. No necessary accommodation remains, so expansion assignment and divided authorship are unnecessary. The signed lead adds one count to Thomas, bringing him to 3.

## 1998-01-26 ? Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, No. 96-795; D.C. Circuit judgment, 83 F.3d 1483.

Source: allentown_mack_sales_service_inc_v_nlrb_merits_1998-01-26.md

### Event

Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, No. 96-795. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued October 15, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, 83 F.3d 1483 (1996), which enforced the Board's unfair-labor-practice and bargaining order. The question concerns the evidence an employer must possess before polling employees about an incumbent union's majority support, including the asserted substitution of proof of lost support for the announced reasonable-doubt standard.

Allentown rehired 32 of 45 former unit employees after purchasing the branch. The successor finding and appropriate unit are unchallenged. The ALJ found the procedural polling safeguards satisfied but found insufficient objective doubt. The later secret ballot was 19–13 against representation; that result does not establish what management knew when it announced the poll.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | All eight other Justices | Common posture and record | Part I |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Rejects facial challenge | Part II |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and denial of enforcement | Parts III–IV: reach, application framework, evidence and mandate |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Leaves the independent remedy objection unreviewed | Part V; Stone-Zsela agrees only in that nonreview disposition |
| Concurrence in Parts I and III–IV and the judgment, dissent from Part II | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports denial of enforcement and also would invalidate the threshold | Statutory ground and its preserved limits |
| Concurrence in Parts I–II and V, dissent from Parts III–IV and judgment | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm enforcement | Question-scope objection and independent substantial-evidence defense |

### Assignment statements

The legal divisions require a signed plenary merits opinion with distinct parts, plus a statutory concurrence/dissent and an application dissent. This form was selected before counts. Part I states common posture and record; Part II sustains the facial rule; Part III reaches and states the actual-standard inquiry; Part IV applies it and directs denial of enforcement; Part V leaves the independently unpreserved objection unreviewed under §10(e). Each part's legal ground and scope, rather than workload, defines its joins.
The actual tables in the [twenty-four-event extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md) and the [Lee](lee_v_harcleroad_threshold_1998-01-20.md), [Buchanan](buchanan_v_angelone_merits_1998-01-21.md), [LaChance/McManus](lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md) and [Lunding](lunding_v_new_york_tax_appeals_tribunal_merits_1998-01-21.md) Records were independently recounted. ## 1998-01-26 ? Brogan v. United States, No. 96-1579; review of United States v. Wiener, 96 F.3d 35.

Source: brogan_v_united_states_merits_1998-01-26.md

### Event

Brogan v. United States, No. 96-1579. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued December 2, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit, United States v. Wiener, 96 F.3d 35 (1996). The question is whether the version of 18 U.S.C. §1001 governing Brogan's October 4, 1993 statement excludes a bare false denial of wrongdoing during federal investigative questioning.

Department of Labor and Internal Revenue Service agents made an unannounced visit to Brogan's home. He agreed to answer and denied receiving cash or gifts from JRD Management. The agents already had payment records; only afterward did they reveal those records and warn that lying to investigators was criminal. He did not change his answer, and no further questions were asked. A jury convicted him of the false statement and unlawful employer payments. The latter conviction is outside this review.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter | Supports affirmance | Parts I–II, III-A and IV: coverage, privilege boundary, contextual interpretation, limits and mandate |
| Same opinion | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports affirmance | Part III-B(1): potential abuse does not supply the categorical statutory exception; actual abuse remains undecided |
| Noncontrolling portion of principal opinion | Thomas | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports affirmance on an additional explanatory ground | Part III-B(2): the proposed doctrine may not prevent eliciting an elaborated fabrication; four votes only |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | Souter | Supports affirmance | Separate statutory-coverage agreement, crime-generation concern, fault reservations and call for congressional attention |
| Statement concurring in part and in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Joins the principal opinion except all of III-B and joins Ginsburg's concurrence |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse as to the §1001 conviction | Bounded statutory exception based on intended coverage, purpose and established practice |

Ginsburg agrees with the narrow no-exception proposition through her separate opinion; she does not join the principal opinion. Stone-Zsela joins the limited abuse response in III-B(1), but not III-B(2). Souter's exclusion concerns the discrete abuse response, not the rest of the statutory or constitutional analysis.

### Assignment statements

Before considering counts, the supported legal divisions require a signed plenary lead with partial joins, Ginsburg's judgment concurrence joined by Souter, Souter's short partial-join statement, and Stevens's dissent joined by Breyer. Parts I–II state the reviewed question, statutory coverage and privilege boundary; III-A treats contextual interpretation and established practice; III-B(1) rejects a categorical exemption based on possible abuse; III-B(2) gives the four Associates' further elaborated-fabrication response; IV preserves limits and affirms. The extra response is an argument, not a separately established constitutional rule or a second judgment.
The actual opinion tables in the [twenty-four-event extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md), the completed [Lee](lee_v_harcleroad_threshold_1998-01-20.md), [Buchanan](buchanan_v_angelone_merits_1998-01-21.md), [LaChance/McManus](lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md), [Lunding](lunding_v_new_york_tax_appeals_tribunal_merits_1998-01-21.md) and [Allentown](allentown_mack_sales_service_inc_v_nlrb_merits_1998-01-26.md) Records were freshly recounted for this assignment. The counted leads are Bates/Foster/Clanton—Ginsburg; Khan/Smith/Allentown—Scalia; Jeffries/Valot/LaChance—Souter; Rybar/Joiner/Rouse—Kennedy; Parker/Richenberg/Buchanan—O'Connor; Salinas/Chicago/Lunding—Thomas; Trest/Baker—Stevens; Hudson/Bay Area—Breyer; Tanford/Fink—Stone-Zsela. Brown, Monroe, Jefferson, Rogers and Lee are excluded; Lee is a reasoned threshold decision. A multipart lead counts once. Last counted event: Allentown, January 26, first alphabetically, by Scalia.
| Justice | Count before Brogan |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 2 |
| Stevens | 2 |
| O'Connor | 3 |
| Scalia | 3 |
| Kennedy | 3 |
| Souter | 3 |
| Thomas | 3 |
| Ginsburg | 3 |
| Breyer | 2 |
The Chief assigns from the seven-Justice affirmance coalition. For I–II, III-A and IV, eligible authors are Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; minimum 2 and all six in the minimum-plus-one pool. For III-B(1), eligible authors and the minimum-2-plus-one pool are Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. The further III-B(2) ground has O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas as its full supporters, each count 3. Ginsburg's judgment-only concurrence creates no principal-opinion eligibility; neither Stone's narrower abuse response nor Souter's coverage join supports that further paragraph.

## 1998-01-26 ? Oubre v. Entergy Operations, Inc., No. 96-1291.

Source: oubre_v_entergy_operations_inc_merits_1998-01-26.md

### Event

Oubre v. Entergy Operations, Inc., No. 96-1291. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued November 12, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Fifth Circuit, 112 F.3d 787, which affirmed summary judgment barring Oubre's ADEA action because she retained consideration paid for a concededly deficient release. Review concerns statutory waiver validity, asserted ratification and prior tender, with separate monetary accounting and discrimination merits reserved.

### Opinion Topology

O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Breyer concurs, joined by O'Connor; both fully join the Court. Scalia and Thomas each dissent separately and join neither the Court nor one another's dissent. Scalia would affirm on lack of tender alone and rejects ratification here; Thomas would affirm on ratification and also preserves tender as a distinct condition.

### Assignment statements

One signed Opinion of the Court resolves the statutory defense and bounded remedy, with the three separate writings needed to preserve the actual disagreements. That form and those governing propositions were fixed before counting.
The actual lead-opinion tables in the [twenty-four-event extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md) and the completed [Lee](lee_v_harcleroad_threshold_1998-01-20.md), [Buchanan](buchanan_v_angelone_merits_1998-01-21.md), [LaChance/McManus](lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md), [Lunding](lunding_v_new_york_tax_appeals_tribunal_merits_1998-01-21.md), [Allentown](allentown_mack_sales_service_inc_v_nlrb_merits_1998-01-26.md) and [Brogan](brogan_v_united_states_merits_1998-01-26.md) Records were recounted from scratch. Counted leads are Ginsburg—Bates/Foster/Clanton; Scalia—Khan/Smith/Allentown; Souter—Jeffries/Valot/LaChance; Kennedy—Rybar/Joiner/Rouse; O'Connor—Parker/Richenberg/Buchanan; Thomas—Salinas/Chicago/Lunding/Brogan; Stevens—Trest/Baker; Breyer—Hudson/Bay Area; Stone-Zsela—Tanford/Fink. Each multipart lead counts once. Brown's filing order, Monroe's summary disposition, Jefferson's jurisdictional dismissal, Rogers's DIG and Lee's signed threshold decision do not count. Last counted event: Brogan, January 26, by Thomas. The same-day alphabetical order is Allentown, Brogan, Oubre, South Dakota; that sequence creates no substantive peer-law dependency.
| Justice | Count before Oubre |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 2 |
| Stevens | 2 |
| O'Connor | 3 |
| Scalia | 3 |
| Kennedy | 3 |
| Souter | 3 |
| Thomas | 4 |
| Ginsburg | 3 |
| Breyer | 2 |
| Writing | Author | Exact joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, in full | Seven direct votes for the statutory rule, its limits and reversal/remand; one counted signed plenary merits lead |
| Concurrence | Breyer | O'Connor | Both fully join the Court; additional voidability and reciprocal-remedy explanation is noncontrolling |
| Dissent | Scalia | None | Would affirm on tender alone; rejects ratification here; joins neither other writing |
| Dissent | Thomas | None | Would affirm on knowing-retention ratification and separately tender; no join in Scalia's contrary ratification analysis |
Breyer and O'Connor's published noncontrolling position treats the deficient arrangement as voidable at the protected employee's election, avoiding an employer's right to cancel benefits through its own noncompliance when the worker does not sue. Suit can permit a properly supported request concerning reciprocal obligations or accounting, without establishing success or a prefiling tender condition. Scalia's published noncontrolling position preserves restoration before repudiation but rejects ratification where discovering the absent safeguards does not cure them. Thomas's published noncontrolling position preserves later knowing ratification of a voidable bargain and the distinct restoration rule; it does not treat original execution as compliant. Each position applies to this statutory and retained-consideration setting, not every waiver or equitable defense.

## 1998-01-26 ? South Dakota v. Yankton Sioux Tribe, No. 96-1581.

Source: south_dakota_v_yankton_sioux_tribe_merits_1998-01-26.md

### Event

South Dakota v. Yankton Sioux Tribe, No. 96-1581. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued December 8, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eighth Circuit, 99 F.3d 1439, which affirmed a declaration that the 1894 Act left the reservation's exterior boundary unchanged and that the waste-site tract lies within it. Review concerns the ceded, unallotted lands and the declaration's continuing consequences after EPA permitted a compacted-clay liner.

The Tribe did not appeal the separate denial of its injunction and rejection of its asserted regulation of this non-Indian activity. The Waste District supported the State without appealing after construction could proceed. The existing state permit and operative reservation-status declaration remain the subject of the controversy.

### Opinion Topology

Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine adopt the three holdings and their qualifications. No separate writing issues.

### Assignment statements

The opinion's form was fixed before counting: one signed plenary Opinion of the Court states the live-controversy application, ceded-land diminishment with complete Article XVIII analysis, and the bounded remedy. All nine fully support every proposition; no distinct qualification calls for a separate writing. No published noncontrolling proposition is created.
The actual lead-opinion tables were freshly recounted from the [twenty-four-event extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md), the completed [Lee](lee_v_harcleroad_threshold_1998-01-20.md), [Buchanan](buchanan_v_angelone_merits_1998-01-21.md), [LaChance/McManus](lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md), [Lunding](lunding_v_new_york_tax_appeals_tribunal_merits_1998-01-21.md), [Allentown](allentown_mack_sales_service_inc_v_nlrb_merits_1998-01-26.md), [Brogan](brogan_v_united_states_merits_1998-01-26.md), and the now-released [Oubre](oubre_v_entergy_operations_inc_merits_1998-01-26.md) Record. Counted leads are Ginsburg—Bates/Foster/Clanton; Scalia—Khan/Smith/Allentown; Souter—Jeffries/Valot/LaChance; Kennedy—Rybar/Joiner/Rouse; O'Connor—Parker/Richenberg/Buchanan/Oubre; Thomas—Salinas/Chicago/Lunding/Brogan; Stevens—Trest/Baker; Breyer—Hudson/Bay Area; Stone-Zsela—Tanford/Fink. Multipart leads count once. Brown, Monroe, Jefferson, Rogers and Lee are excluded procedural, summary or threshold events. The last counted event is Oubre, January 26, by O'Connor; its actual table, not a predicted assignment, supplies that count. Same-day alphabetical assignment order creates no peer-law dependency.
| Justice | Count before South Dakota v. Yankton Sioux Tribe |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 2 |
| Stevens | 2 |
| O'Connor | 4 |
| Scalia | 3 |
| Kennedy | 3 |
| Souter | 3 |
| Thomas | 4 |
| Ginsburg | 3 |
| Breyer | 2 |
| Writing | Author | Exact joins | Authority and count |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, in full | Nine direct votes for all holdings, qualifications and reversal/remand; one signed plenary merits lead |
The live-controversy, complete statutory-construction and bounded-remedy propositions all have the same nine-Justice authority. No separate writing, judgment-only join, partial join or Marks question arises.
The mandate reverses the Eighth Circuit's reservation-status judgment and remands for judgment recognizing that the ceded, unallotted settler lands, including this supported site, are outside the reservation. South Dakota has primary territorial jurisdiction at that site. That consequence does not decide every federal or tribal authority, private title, dependent-community or unextinguished-allotment predicate. Total disestablishment, allotments, trust lands, Article VIII governmental parcels and later reacquired land are reserved. Remaining parcel-specific and statutory jurisdiction questions may be addressed below as actually presented. The Tribe's unappealed injunction/regulatory rejection stays undisturbed; the EPA waiver stands, no liner is ordered, and no hearing, survey, new enforcement action, deadline or retained jurisdiction is created.

McCardle v. Haddad; Second Circuit Nos. 96-9133(L), 96-9503. Plenary merits decision, January 29, 1998. On review of 131 F.3d 43, decided November 21, 1997. The question is whether Haddad preserved qualified immunity as a ground for postverdict judgment as a matter of law on the unlawful-search claim. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is established.

The jury rejected the stop claim, found an unlawful search and awarded no compensatory damages. The court entered $1 nominal damages, refused a punitive instruction and awarded $0.33 in fees and costs. The Court of Appeals affirmed. The stop verdict was not appealed by McCardle; the distinct punitive and fee rulings are outside this review.

Ginsburg delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. The same nine Justices adopt the preservation rule, its application, qualifications and bounded affirmance.

Before considering counts, the case warrants one signed plenary merits opinion resolving the Rule 50 question and its limited application. It is not a jurisdictional dismissal, summary action or per curiam order. The common preservation rule and no-remand disposition require no divided authorship or separate opinion.
The actual opinion tables in the [twenty-four-event entering extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md) and all eight completed Chunk 3 Records were freshly recounted. ## 1998-02-02 ? Alexander v. Whitman; Third Circuit No. 95-5414; review of 114 F.3d 1392. No separate Supreme Court docket is supplied.

Source: alexander_v_whitman_merits_1998-02-02.md

### Event

Alexander v. Whitman; Third Circuit No. 95-5414. Plenary merits decision, February 2, 1998. On review of 114 F.3d 1392, decided May 23, 1997, affirming a Rule 12(b)(6) dismissal. Review concerns Fourteenth Amendment personhood before live birth and the mother's constitutional challenge to New Jersey's live-birth line for wrongful-death and survival remedies. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is established.

Alexander alleges that her child was stillborn after a near-term pregnancy and that the surrogate refused estate letters needed for identified claims in her state malpractice action. Her personal state claim seeks emotional-distress relief. Factual allegations, including prenatal pain, are accepted at this stage; negligence, causation, the exact death mechanism and damages are not found. The federal money-damages dismissal was not appealed, no class was certified, and the lawyers' distinct personal and representative claims are outside this review.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, in full | Supports every reviewed affirmance and the bounded mandate | Parts I–V |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Souter | Stone-Zsela | Supports every reviewed affirmance; agrees in reaching the authorized claims and in the maternal-liberty dismissal without joining Parts I or III | Part II's personhood rule; Parts IV-A and IV-B's separate equality rulings; Part V's mandate |

### Assignment statements

Before any count, the legal subjects and join boundaries require one signed plenary merits opinion: Part I addresses claimant-specific power and authorized reach; Part II applies Roe's personhood rule; Part III decides the distinct maternal-liberty claim; Parts IV-A and IV-B separately decide wrongful-death and survival equality; Part V states the bounded affirmance and exclusions. These divisions preserve actual issues rather than alter eligibility or count. A single fully eligible author can carry all parts, and no separate writing or divided lead is needed.
Actual opinion tables in the [twenty-four-event extract](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK3_ENTERING_ASSIGNMENTS.md), all eight January 20–26 Records and the registered [McCardle Record](mccardle_v_haddad_merits_1998-01-29.md) were recounted. Each multipart lead counts once. Brown, Monroe, Jefferson, Rogers and Lee are excluded procedural, summary or threshold events. The last counted event is McCardle, January 29, by Ginsburg. There are twenty-eight counted leads.
| Justice | Count before assignment |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 2 |
| Stevens | 3 |

## 1998-02-09 ? United States v. Fields / Crawley; Second Circuit Nos. 96-1168 and 96-1257, 113 F.3d 313. No separate Supreme Court docket is supplied.

Source: united_states_v_fields_merits_1998-02-09.md

### Event

United States v. Fields / Crawley; Second Circuit Nos. 96-1168 and 96-1257, 113 F.3d 313. Plenary merits decision, February 9, 1998, reviewing the Second Circuit's May 12, 1997 affirmance of both convictions. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is supplied. The question concerns observation through a five-to-six-inch gap below bedroom blinds from a shared side yard, with each defendant's personal privacy interest and the necessary entry, announcement and suppression consequences. Informant disclosure, cocaine sentencing, Fields's fine and jury selection remain outside review.

Fields repeatedly paid for access, possessed a key and could enter and bring guests; Crawley repeatedly visited with permission but had no key, payment or established overnight plan that evening. Warner respected the closed bedroom door. Officers could not see inside from the street or sidewalk. They crossed fenced rear and side areas, shared with other tenants, and saw the well-lit room without enhancement, leaning or tiptoes. After observation and without seeking a warrant, they arranged front and rear teams. A porch occupant warned of police before the planned front knock. Officers then forced the common-building rear door and entered the apartment through its open door. A later warrant and Warner's signed consent followed while the premises were secured.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment, in part the opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg in full | Supports limited vacatur/remand | Parts I–V |
| Same opinion, partial joins | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela | Supports vacatur/remand on distinct actual-search and entry grounds | I-A, I-B and II-A; additionally agrees in IV's specified allocation of suppression, consent, independence, attenuation and later-warrant consequences without final suppression, and in the formal judgment only of V |
| Same opinion, partial joins | O'Connor | Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Oppose vacatur/remand; would affirm | I-A, III-A, III-B and IV |
| Same opinion, partial joins | O'Connor | Scalia | Opposes vacatur/remand; would affirm | I-A, III-B and IV; not III-A |
| Concurrence in Parts I and II-A and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | — | Finds an actual search and unjustified police-created exigency; remands remedies | Does not join the plurality's unresolved-application rationale or the new review-method extension |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer join Part I only | Would affirm on actual shared exposure and specific entry/announcement grounds | Part I: substantive application; Part II: Scalia alone objects to the new exigency-review allocation |

I-A has nine votes; I-B and II-A have five. III-A has seven Associates, and III-B and IV have all eight Associates. No unified controlling search or entry application is supplied by combining those separate rationales.

### Assignment statements

Before counting, the legal divisions require one signed lead announcing the judgment and speaking for the Court in its majority-supported portions, together with Stone's concurrence and Scalia's substantive/method dissent. III-A adopts the seven-Associate review extension; III-B preserves the distinct existing entry and announcement standards without resolving this application. IV states conditional remedial law. V explains the four remanders' disposition rationale. These divisions follow legal support, not workload.

## 1998-02-25 ? Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government; No. 96-1577.

Source: alaska_v_native_village_of_venetie_tribal_government_merits_1998-02-25.md

### Event

Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, No. 96-1577. Plenary merits decision, February 25, 1998; argued December 10, 1997. On writ of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Ninth Circuit, 101 F.3d 1286. Review concerns whether the former Venetie Reservation lands conveyed under the Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act are a dependent Indian community under 18 U.S.C. §1151(b), supplying the territorial authority asserted for the nonmember school-construction tax. The District Court rejected that classification in Alaska’s action to enjoin collection; the Court of Appeals reversed.

### Opinion Topology

Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine adopt both holdings and the bounded disposition. No separate writing issues.

### Assignment statements

**Form fixed first:** One signed plenary merits opinion for both statutory propositions and their bounded application. See `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_B_FORM_FIRST.md`; governing propositions were fixed before counts or an author.
**Assignment authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns from the unanimous reversal coalition.
**Before-assignment counts:** Stone-Zsela 3; Stevens 4; O'Connor 5; Scalia 4; Kennedy 4; Souter 4; Thomas 4; Ginsburg 4; Breyer 4.
**Last counted event:** Rivet v. Regions Bank of Louisiana, No. 96-1971.; 1998-02-24; Scalia.
**Eligible authors:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Balance pool:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The pool contains eligible authors no more than one above the eligible minimum.
**Author selected:** Stevens. Stevens’s actual Yankton Sioux opinion supplies the closest concrete fit: it reads an entire congressional land transaction, addresses the apparently contrary savings terms and keeps title, territory and the geographic mandate distinct. The same work is needed to explain why the section 1618(b) election and section 1636(d) asset protections do not preserve either territorial predicate. Stone’s approved distinction between tribal identity and territorial authority is a strong alternative at a lower count, but Stevens’s directly demonstrated whole-act treatment of the present Indian-country boundary supplies a specific fit advantage. No unresolved formulation objection requires expansion or a changed commitment.
**Recount evidence:** `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_VENETIE_ASSIGNMENT_EVIDENCE.json` copies actual preceding Record opinion topologies; `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_VENETIE_COUNT_RECEIPT.json` records the individually reviewed classifications. All current signed plenary merits leads are recounted from those actual topologies, including earlier Records in this Run; no prior assignment tally supplies a count. This signed merits lead counts once for Stevens; no separate writing counts.

Plenary merits decision, February 25, 1998; argued October 6, 1997. On writs of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the D.C. Circuit, 90 F.3d 525. The paired cases concern the banks’ right to seek APA review and whether §109 of the Federal Credit Union Act, 12 U.S.C. §1759, permits one federal credit union to add multiple unrelated occupational groups. The Court of Appeals allowed the challenge and rejected the agency’s construction after the District Court had sustained it.

### Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg. Its Article III, statutory-zone and occupational common-bond conclusions have those five direct joins. O’Connor dissents, joined by Stevens, Souter and Breyer, on the statutory zone of interests. The dissent reaches no common-bond merits question. No legislative-history rationale or conditional merits opinion is published.

### Assignment statements

**Form fixed first:** One signed plenary merits lead for both paired dockets, with a separate statutory-zone dissent. See `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_B_FORM_FIRST.md`; governing propositions were fixed before counts or an author.
**Assignment authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns from the five-Justice affirmance coalition.
**Before-assignment counts:** Stone-Zsela 3; Stevens 5; O'Connor 5; Scalia 4; Kennedy 4; Souter 4; Thomas 4; Ginsburg 4; Breyer 4.
**Last counted event:** Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government; No. 96-1577.; 1998-02-25; Stevens.
**Eligible authors:** Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg.
**Balance pool:** Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg. The pool contains eligible authors no more than one above the eligible minimum.
**Author selected:** Stone-Zsela. Stone writes for concrete fit in coordinating the precise source of review with a statutory boundary the agency cannot replace. His actual MCI opinion separates interpretation within a delegated domain from elimination of an enacted requirement, and his approved current position supplies the same disciplined distinction between provision-connected injury and guaranteed merits success. Scalia is the strongest alternative through Bennett and MCI; both can express the common text-and-structure ground faithfully. Stone has the lower count, three to four, and his existing authored boundary formulation directly fits the occupational-membership issue. No legislative-history assertion, authorship-induced join or conditional merits vote is needed. O'Connor authors the threshold dissent because her frozen ground precisely distinguishes cooperative membership structure from market exclusion; Stevens, Souter and Breyer join that actual common ground, without publication of their unadopted conditional merits views.

The action seeks damages under 42 U.S.C. §1983 against Puerto Rico officials and under Bivens and §1331 against federal officials. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is assigned. **Form fixed first:** One signed plenary merits opinion preserving ten distinct claim and procedural components; a Stone partial concurrence. See `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_B_FORM_FIRST.md`; governing propositions were fixed before counts or an author.

Ginsburg writes for the combined work shown in her Clanton and Elder opinions: identifying personally responsible material warrant deception, retaining the complete conduct-date defense, and preventing a legal-source shorthand from becoming a Supreme-Court-only notice rule. Her McCardle opinion also distinguishes proper presentation from legal merits, directly fitting the untimely and waived components. Scalia is the strongest equal-count alternative through Smith's obvious-force analysis, but this record requires coordinating that distinct force limit with the affidavit and appellate-component boundaries, which Ginsburg's actual opinions address together. Source: shahar_v_bowers_merits_1998-03-02.md

Shahar v. Bowers, Eleventh Circuit No. 93-9345, 114 F.3d 1097 (en banc). Plenary merits decision on submission, March 2, 1998. The case concerns withdrawal in July 1991 of an accepted civilian staff-attorney offer because of a planned same-sex religious commitment ceremony. The preserved theories are intimate association, expressive association, free exercise and equal protection. Shahar seeks damages and injunctive employment relief. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is supplied; substantive due process and an independent free-speech claim are outside this review.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| Concurrence in setting aside the equality judgment and remanding; dissent from formal vacatur and the ordinary-rationality mandate | Stone-Zsela | — | Would reverse that claim and remand under intermediate scrutiny | Does not join the Court’s formal equality disposition or mandate, its ordinary-rationality reasoning, or reach the association/free-exercise claims |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | O’Connor | — | Joins the free-exercise remand; would sustain association and equality judgments | Particular professional employment balance and ordinary rationality |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm all four claims | Assumed-right employment balance, secular-ground free-exercise analysis and ordinary rationality |

### Assignment statements

**Form fixed first:** One signed plenary lead with association, free-exercise, equality and bounded-mandate parts, with three substantively distinct separate writings. See `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_B_FORM_FIRST.md`; governing propositions were fixed before counts or an author.
**Assignment authority:** Stevens assigns as the senior Associate in the actual vacatur and bounded-remand coalitions. Stone expressly does not reach the association and free-exercise claims and proposes a distinct formal reversal and intermediate-scrutiny equality instruction, rather than the five-Justice vacatur/ordinary-rationality mandate. His common preference to set aside the equality defense judgment is not converted into a join in that different formal mandate. The Chief's balance policy does not bind this Associate assignment; actual counts, eligibility and the corresponding pool are nevertheless recorded.
**Before-assignment counts:** Stone-Zsela 4; Stevens 5; O'Connor 5; Scalia 4; Kennedy 4; Souter 4; Thomas 4; Ginsburg 5; Breyer 4.
**Last counted event:** Aponte Matos v. Toledo Dávila; First Circuit No. 97-1645.; 1998-02-26; Ginsburg.

**Balance pool:** Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. The pool contains eligible authors no more than one above the eligible minimum.
**Author selected:** Souter. Souter writes for the concrete coordination demonstrated in his Umbehr, Jeffries and Romer opinions: actual protected activity, a justified employment restriction, personal causal proof and the independent ordinary-equality inquiry. The separate Brown/Lukumi religion ground must remain distinct rather than merging every right into Pickering. Breyer is the strongest equal-count alternative through his full Jeffries/Brown joins and actual-function analysis; Souter's authored treatment of the specific employment/equality boundaries supplies the closer fit. All five common joiners can carry every part; O'Connor can join the free-exercise part but not the whole lead. Stevens assigns the complete opinion to one full supporter, without artificial splitting. O'Connor's own separate statement preserves her differentiated association/equality position; Scalia states the common assumed-right and secular-ground dissent with Thomas. Stone's equality concurrence preserves his approved proposed standard and express nonreach.

## 1998-03-03 ? Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach; No. 96-1482.

Source: lexecon_inc_v_milberg_weiss_bershad_hynes_lerach_merits_1998-03-03.md

### Event

Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach, No. 96-1482. Plenary merits decision, March 3, 1998; argued November 10, 1997. On writ of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Ninth Circuit. The question is whether an MDL transferee court may use 28 U.S.C. §1404(a) to assign a transferred action to itself for trial despite §1407(a)’s remand command. Lexecon’s Illinois diversity tort action was transferred to Arizona for coordinated pretrial proceedings. The Arizona court entered pretrial dispositions, denied requests to suggest remand and retained the defamation claim for a defense trial judgment; the Ninth Circuit sustained the self-transfer. Lexecon preserved the objection through remand requests, mandamus and direct appeal.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reverses and remands | All eight Associate Justices join Parts I–III; Stone-Zsela joins Parts I–II on statutory return and the trial-forum remedy, and agrees only with Part III’s ancillary dispositions |

The opinion rests on text, structure and the preserved objection. It adds no legislative-history rationale. No separate writing issues.

### Assignment statements

**Form fixed first:** One signed plenary statutory lead on mandatory MDL return, the preserved trial-forum remedy and distinct ancillary pretrial/appellate dispositions. See `freeze/OT_1997CHUNK4_B_FORM_FIRST.md`; governing propositions were fixed before counts or an author.
**Assignment authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns from the unanimous reversal/remand coalition; his Part III disposition-only agreement creates assignment authority but not full-lead authorship eligibility.
**Before-assignment counts:** Stone-Zsela 4; Stevens 5; O'Connor 5; Scalia 4; Kennedy 4; Souter 5; Thomas 5; Ginsburg 5; Breyer 4.
**Last counted event:** Bogan v. Scott-Harris; No. 96-1569.; 1998-03-03; Thomas.
**Eligible authors:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Balance pool:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The pool contains eligible authors no more than one above the eligible minimum.
**Author selected:** Kennedy. Kennedy writes for the concrete work of connecting the particular mandatory duty with its proper remedy while preserving authorized pretrial adjudication. His Good Real Property opinion distinguishes a duty from an invented substantive dismissal sanction; his Caterpillar join preserves actual cure rather than efficiency as the ground protecting a completed judgment. O'Connor, who authored Caterpillar, is the strongest alternative. Both can explain the uncured forum defect and preserve the pretrial rulings faithfully, and Kennedy has the lower count, four to five. Stone fully supports Parts I-II but not every independent ancillary rationale in Part III; a separate author part is unnecessary because the eight eligible Associates can carry the entire opinion. Scalia joins the actual textual/structural ground; no legislative-history rationale is added or inferred.

## 1998-03-09 ? Lewis v. United States, No. 96-7151.

Source: lewis_v_united_states_merits_1998-03-09.md

### Event

Lewis v. United States, No. 96-7151. Plenary merits decision, March 9, 1998; argued November 12, 1997. On certiorari to the Fifth Circuit, the Court considers whether the Assimilative Crimes Act incorporates Louisiana's child-victim first-degree-murder offense when federal murder law already punishes the killing. Lewis was convicted for the killing of her four-year-old stepdaughter at an Army base in Louisiana. The Fifth Circuit rejected assimilation, retained her conviction as federal second-degree murder on the jury's necessary findings, and affirmed life imprisonment as within the federal maximum. Lewis does not contest retention of the federal conviction; she seeks federal resentencing.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship to judgment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I: ACA inquiry and nonassimilation | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Six Justices adopt the governing framework and its application. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II: retained conviction and federal resentencing | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Eight Justices adopt the bounded conviction treatment and sentence-only remand. |
| Opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Both join Part II; they agree with Part I's judgment on a distinct generic-offense construction. |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would allow assimilation and deny the requested federal resentencing. |

### Assignment statements

The form-first receipt, freeze/OT_1997CHUNK5_RESUMPTION_FORM_FIRST.md, fixes a single signed plenary lead opinion with two doctrinally necessary parts, a Scalia concurrence joined by Thomas, and a Kennedy dissent before counts or authors were considered. One eligible author can write both parts; dividing authorship is unnecessary.
Stone assigns because he joins the judgment coalition on both components. The scratch recount from actual current-term opinion tables counts 45 preceding signed plenary lead opinions, with same-date cases ordered alphabetically. Counts before assignment: Stone-Zsela 4; Stevens 5; O'Connor 6; Scalia 4; Kennedy 5; Souter 5; Thomas 5; Ginsburg 6; Breyer 5. The last counted event is Gray v. Maryland, March 9, 1998, authored by O'Connor. The same-day procedural Glendora action does not count; Quality King follows Lewis alphabetically and is excluded. Existing later-written or later-effective assignments are not reopened.
Whole-opinion eligible authors: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, each joining every proposition in both parts. Scalia and Thomas cannot carry Part I's framework; Kennedy does not join the judgment. Minimum eligible count is 4. The balance pool is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer. Breyer is selected for fit: his frozen ground expressly works through the ACA's supplementary operation and the practical comparison between comprehensive federal grading and genuinely distinct state conduct. This is the principal general-rule task, and his formulation preserves its boundaries without converting it into mechanical element comparison or unconstrained policy balancing. Stone is a strong alternative on concrete offense/remedy separation and is fully eligible; Breyer's more developed articulation of the general statutory comparison supplies the case-specific advantage. No expansion accommodation is needed and no vote is changed to justify authorship. Breyer's counted total becomes 6; every other total is unchanged. Separate writings do not count. Exact source-row evidence is preserved with the RESUMPTION assignment recount.

## 1998-03-24 ? Almendarez-Torres v. United States, No. 96-6839.

Source: almendarez_torres_v_united_states_merits_1998-03-24.md

Almendarez-Torres v. United States, No. 96-6839. Argued October 14, 1997; decided March 24, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, reviewing its affirmance of an 85-month sentence. The question is whether the prior aggravated-felony conviction in 8 U.S.C. §1326(b)(2) is an element of aggravated unlawful reentry or a sentencing factor that need not appear in the indictment.

The September 1995 indictment charged unlawful reentry under §1326 without alleging the prior aggravated-felony convictions. At his December 1995 plea, Almendarez-Torres admitted the prior convictions and deportation; he preserved his objection that the indictment authorized no more than §1326(a)'s two-year maximum. The District Court imposed the enhanced sentence in March 1996.

The questions concern due process in Ohio's capital-clemency procedure and whether the offered interview compels self-incrimination.

Woodard brought this §1983 action after receiving notice of a voluntary interview and a clemency hearing. He did not request the interview. The District Court granted judgment on the pleadings. The Sixth Circuit rejected an independent entitlement to clemency, but ordered further proceedings on an established-process theory of due process and on a possible unconstitutional condition on silence.

## 1998-03-31 ? United States v. Scheffer, No. 96-1133.

Source: united_states_v_scheffer_merits_1998-03-31.md

### Event

United States v. Scheffer, No. 96-1133. Argued November 3, 1997; decided March 31, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces under 28 U.S.C. §1259, reviewing 44 M.J. 442. The question is whether Military Rule of Evidence 707's categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence violates the accused's constitutional right to present a defense.

An Air Force investigator administered the examination before the urinalysis result was known. The examiner considered Scheffer's answers to show no deception. At his court-martial, Scheffer denied knowing drug ingestion and sought the examiner's testimony to support his credibility after impeachment. The judge excluded it under Rule 707 without an individualized reliability hearing. The military appellate court removed that categorical obstacle on constitutional grounds; it did not hold this proffer necessarily admissible.

United States v. Hinds; Fourth Circuit No. 97-4030, district case CR-96-148. Plenary merits decision, April 6, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 6. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the Fourth Circuit's unpublished October 15, 1997 judgment affirming the §844(i) conviction. The questions are whether the Pleasant Ridge United Church of Christ building was used in interstate commerce or an activity affecting commerce under that statute and, if the statute reaches it, whether that application lies within Congress's Commerce Clause authority. Hinds also presented an Establishment Clause objection below. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established.

Hinds and an accomplice burned the church in Greensboro, North Carolina. Hinds waived a jury and stipulated the elements other than the commerce connection. After hearing evidence on that issue, the District Court denied his motion to dismiss and found him guilty. The Fourth Circuit recited the property-use element, held the evidence sufficient, and rejected the religious objection. The church sent annual funds through its national denomination, employed one full-time and two part-time workers, provided the full-time employee insurance procured from out-of-state companies, and purchased utilities, insurance, religious materials and other goods and services.
