Source: carriger_v_stewart_merits_1998-04-16.md

### Event

**Carriger v. Stewart**, Ninth Circuit No. 95-99025, 132 F.3d 463; merits decision, **April 16, 1998**. Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) concerns the Ninth Circuit's December 17, 1997 en banc judgment reversing the district court and directing habeas relief requiring a new trial. The federal application was filed in August 1995. The district court treated the new claims as abuse of the writ. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established; the matter is decided on the merits submission.

The questions are whether the lower court used the correct innocence-gateway and state-finding rules, which statutory provisions govern this pending capital application, and whether its Brady new-trial instruction can stand. The suppression claim concerns the corrections file of the State's central witness, Robert Dunbar. His later confessions and repudiation, the state credibility finding, disputed physical corroboration, and evidence of possible joint participation bear on the gateway. The underlying constitutional claim, the gateway permitting its review, and a freestanding innocence claim remain distinct.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing or portion | Author | Joins | Relationship to judgment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I: pending capital application's statutory treatment | Stevens | O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Five adopt the bounded Lindh extension; Stone-Zsela supports use of former §2254(d)(8) in the mandate without joining this temporal-construction rationale |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II: gateway and state-finding correction | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Five require renewed correct analysis; no finding that the gateway now succeeds or fails |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III: distinct Brady elements and conditional full-file materiality | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Six support the conditional merits instruction; it establishes neither suppression nor gateway passage |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV: bounded remand and reserved acquisition question | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Five preserve both merits theories through a lawful route and adopt the complete mandate |
| Concurrence | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins Parts II–IV and the complete remand; explains why he would have affirmed outright under a bounded extension of Kyles |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join the dissent from Parts II and IV only | O'Connor joins Parts I and III; the four agree to remove the writ instruction but would reject this gateway definitively |

Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join no portion of the lead opinion. They reserve the pending-capital temporal question and do not reach a materiality disposition. Their join in O'Connor's dissent adopts neither her Part I join nor her Part III conditional materiality conclusion. No separate opinion is issued by any of those three Justices.

### Assignment statements

One signed plenary opinion is appropriate because the Court resolves a live gateway/remand dispute, a distinct temporal construction, and conditional merits instructions on full merits submission. Its four parts reflect those legal jobs and the actual joins. A Stone concurrence explains his distinct affirmative theory and preferred relief; an O'Connor partial concurrence/dissent explains definitive gateway rejection. Form, proposition scope and these divisions were fixed before counts or author selection. There is no per curiam classification, count-driven division, separate author for each part, or invented circulation history.
The fresh recount reads each preceding counted lead author from its actual OT1997 opinion table. Before every part's assignment the same 56 preceding opinions yield: **Stone-Zsela 5; Stevens 6; O'Connor 7; Scalia 6; Kennedy 7; Souter 6; Thomas 6; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 6**. The last counted event is **United States v. Bird, April 13, 1998, Kennedy**. Threshold/procedural actions, summaries, per curiams, separate writings, Section 10 explanations and prior stopped Carriger work are excluded. The source-by-source recount is preserved in [the assignment receipt](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK6_CARRIGER_ASSIGNMENT_RECOUNT.json).
The Chief belongs to the operative former-law direction coalition and the complete remand majority and assigns the lead opinion. His judgment agreement on former-law treatment does not make him eligible to author its unsupported temporal rationale. For each part the recorded assignment is:
| Part | Eligible authors | Minimum-plus-one pool | Selected author |
|---|---|---|---|
| I, temporal rationale | Stevens 6, O'Connor 7, Souter 6, Ginsburg 7, Breyer 6 | All five; minimum 6, ceiling 7 | Stevens |
| II, gateway correction | Stone-Zsela 5, Stevens 6, Souter 6, Ginsburg 7, Breyer 6 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Breyer; minimum 5, ceiling 6 | Stevens |
| III, conditional Brady instruction | Stone-Zsela 5, Stevens 6, O'Connor 7, Souter 6, Ginsburg 7, Breyer 6 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Breyer; minimum 5, ceiling 6 | Stevens |
| IV, complete mandate | Stone-Zsela 5, Stevens 6, Souter 6, Ginsburg 7, Breyer 6 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Breyer; minimum 5, ceiling 6 | Stevens |
The common fully eligible authors are Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; the intersection of the four component pools is Stevens, Souter and Breyer. Stevens can therefore deliver every part without divided authorship. **Stevens writes the single lead opinion.** The dominant work is applying the whole-record probability inquiry he authored in Schlup and distinguishing it from statutory deference and Brady materiality. Souter is the strongest alternative because his Kyles opinion supplies the conditional merits rules, but the acquisition extension remains reserved and gateway correction is the principal task. Stone's lower count and concrete ability to separate procedure, merits and relief were considered. Stevens's authored probability rule and his frozen, specifically corrective remand provide the case-specific fit advantage; Stone's separate proposed extension would not improve the accuracy of the reserved Court holding. No accommodation changes any Associate's commitment, so expansion is not the assignment basis. O'Connor authors the dissent from her own frozen correct-standard failure assessment; separate writings do not enter the balance.
Carriger adds one Stevens lead opinion, not one per part. Engine §8's later-committed/earlier-effective rule is applied: this Record enters future applicable counts and reopens no committed assignment. The complete existing Record inventory has no event dated April 16 or later; no later result or author is displaced.

## 1998-04-20 ? Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich; Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-3157 and 96-3159; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

Source: womens_medical_professional_corp_v_voinovich_merits_1998-04-20.md

### Event

Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich; Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-3157 and 96-3159. Plenary merits decision, April 20, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 7. On review of the Sixth Circuit’s final judgment, 130 F.3d 187, entered November 18, 1997. No argument date or separate Supreme Court docket is supplied.

The questions are whether Casey’s abortion-review standard or Salerno’s no-valid-applications formulation governs this facial challenge; whether Ohio’s D&X prohibition fails because of its definition, clinical reach, notice or criminal and civil culpability provisions; and whether postviability law must permit necessary abortion to avert severe, lasting and potentially irreversible mental or emotional injury. The Court addresses the complete Casey standard rather than adopting an independent numerical facial threshold.

Ohio’s H.B.135 created an all-stage method prohibition, a separate postviability prohibition, viability-testing requirements and related clinical duties. The district court’s preliminary injunction became permanent on January 12, 1996 after consolidation with trial by stipulation. The Sixth Circuit sustained an injunction against the whole Act, relying on the method definition’s overlap with D&E, clinical-fault concerns, serious mental-health exclusion and inseverability. Those legal conclusions remain subject to review. The lower standing ruling and the State’s failure to contest it in the court of appeals supply no new standing holding here.

Under §2919.17(A), purposeful performance, inducement or attempted performance or inducement of an abortion on a viable fetus is prohibited unless a physician, acting in good faith and using reasonable medical judgment, determines either that abortion is necessary to prevent maternal death or a serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function, or that the fetus is not viable after the assessment required by §2919.18(A). The latter provision requires, beginning with the twenty-second week, a good-faith determination of nonviability using reasonable medical judgment, after examining the patient and performing or arranging the viability tests a reasonable physician would use. Its medical-emergency exception excuses that testing obligation. The separate twenty-four-week viability presumption is rebuttable.

Section 2919.16(F) defines the emergency through the physician’s good-faith, reasonable medical assessment of a pregnancy complication: immediate abortion must be needed to prevent the patient’s death or to avoid a serious risk, created by delay, of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Section 2919.16(J) separately defines that serious-risk category by a medically diagnosed condition complicating the pregnancy that directly or indirectly causes such impairment. Its nonexclusive examples are preeclampsia, inevitable abortion, prematurely ruptured membranes, diabetes and multiple sclerosis. These enacted categories remain distinct from the constitutional protection and the method-prohibition defenses addressed below.

### Opinion Topology

Justice Souter delivers the opinion of the Court. Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer join it in full; the following partial joins identify every additional supporter.

| Writing or portion | Author | Additional joins beyond the five-Justice whole-opinion coalition | Authority and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Part I — Complete Casey standard | Souter | Stone-Zsela | Six direct joins in the governing framework; this does not make Stone a supporter of the method remand. |
| Part II — Notice and fault | Souter | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Eight direct joins; Stone-Zsela dissents. |
| Part III.A — Necessary mental-health protection | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Kennedy | Seven direct joins in the rule and necessary-care affirmance. |
| Part III.B — Protective construction and health-linked relief | Souter | Kennedy | Six direct joins in the particular construction inquiry and its limits. Stone leaves severability and precise relief below on his own stated ground. |
| Part IV — Method-ban application | Souter | No additional joins | Five direct joins in the substantive remand. |
| Part V.A — Ohio severability principles | Souter | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Eight direct joins in ordinary state-law severability, without agreement on every constitutional disposition. |
| Part V.B — Remaining mandate and interim method protection | Souter | No additional joins | Five direct joins in the bounded unresolved-method and provision-specific mandate. |

Stone-Zsela concurs in part and dissents in part, without joins. Kennedy concurs in part and dissents in part, without joins. Scalia concurs in part and dissents in part, joined by Thomas. Their precise grounds and different remedies appear below. Every controlling portion has a direct majority; no combined rationale under Marks is required.

### Assignment statements

Form precedes author selection: one signed opinion of the Court, with distinct sections for the complete framework, notice/fault, necessary health care, method application and relief; three separate writings explain the Chief’s definitive invalidation position, Kennedy’s narrower regulatory position, and Scalia/Thomas’s retained constitutional objection. Five Associates support every controlling part. A single author can carry the full opinion; no part is divided into another lead to alter eligibility or counts.
Fresh recount from preceding OT1997 opinion tables: Stone-Zsela 5; Stevens 7; O’Connor 7; Scalia 6; Kennedy 7; Souter 6; Thomas 6; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 6. There are 57 counted signed plenary lead opinions. The last counted event is Carriger v. Stewart, April 16, 1998. Threshold-only dispositions, summaries, per curiams, procedural orders, separate writings and stopped matters are excluded. The [recount](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK7_WOMEN_RESUMED_ASSIGNMENT_RECOUNT.json) and [assignment review](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK7_WOMEN_RESUMED_ASSIGNMENT_REVIEW.md) identify the source tables. No later-date opinion enters this count.
The Chief assigns the necessary-care affirmance because he is in that judgment coalition. Stevens, the senior participating Associate in the relevant coalitions, assigns the notice vacatur and method-remand work, including the framework as applied to that remand. Stone’s agreement with the framework alone does not authorize him to assign a remand judgment he opposes. Their component-specific assignment authority produces one Souter opinion. Because the Chief assigns a component, §8’s balance rule governs the single lead. Its fully eligible authors are Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. All five are in the whole-opinion pool: minimum 6, ceiling 7. Stone, Kennedy, Scalia and Thomas cannot carry every part; partial joins do not qualify them for the complete lead. No artificial per-part pool is used to change the single opinion’s eligible set.
Souter is selected on fit. His actual Fargo opinion coordinates the same distinct legal tasks: complete access review, the intelligibility of clinical criteria, separate treatment of fault and a bounded remedial remand. That demonstrated work is useful here without making prior authorship a preference by itself. Breyer, also at six, is the strongest equal-count alternative for practical interference and alternatives; Souter’s demonstrated integration of the notice/fault distinction with the full framework supplies the specific advantage. Stevens’s assignment of the components outside the Chief’s coalition likewise uses Souter’s supported account of these distinctions, not an assumed agreement with Stone’s final invalidation. No expansion bargain or changed commitment is necessary. Counts identify the permissible pool and do not determine law, form or joins.
Under §8, this Record enters counts for assignments made after its preservation when its effective date qualifies, but reopens no already committed assignment. The [later-Record dependency review](../freeze/OT_1997CHUNK7_WOMEN_RESUMED_DEPENDENCY_REVIEW.md) separately examines every existing Record dated on or after April 20. The existing chronological slot, subsequent event dates and completed assignments are preserved.

## 1998-04-21 ? Atlantic Mutual Insurance Co. v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue, No. 97-147.

Source: atlantic_mutual_insurance_co_v_commissioner_merits_1998-04-21.md

### Event

Atlantic Mutual Insurance Co. v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue, No. 97-147. Merits decision, April 21, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 7. Argued March 2, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Third Circuit, which reversed the Tax Court and sustained Treasury's construction of reserve strengthening under the Tax Reform Act of 1986. The question is whether that treatment in the transition to discounted insurance-loss reserves is consistent with the Act. The disputed construction produced a reported $519,987 deficiency; Atlantic's reserve increases did not result from a change in computational methods or assumptions.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I and III | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports affirmance | Statutory interpretive room and permission; specified computation, exceptions, timing, application and limits |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Independently sufficient deference explanation supporting affirmance | Chevron's controlling force for the reasonable construction within that statutory room |

Stone-Zsela joins Parts I and III and the judgment. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

Before counting or choosing an author, the legal work calls for one signed plenary merits opinion: Part I identifies statutory interpretive room and permission; Part II states the sufficient deference ground; Part III preserves the actual computation, exceptions, temporal conditions and bounded application. These divisions reflect different adopted propositions and join scope. They are not separate counted opinions. All nine tentatively support affirmance; all eight Associates support every proposition. Stone supports Parts I and III, not the complete Part II rationale, and is therefore ineligible to deliver the complete opinion.
The Chief assigns because he is in the judgment coalition. A fresh actual-Record opinion-table recount immediately before assignment yielded: Stone-Zsela 5; Stevens 7; O'Connor 7; Scalia 6; Kennedy 7; Souter 6; Thomas 6; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 6. There are 57 counted signed plenary lead opinions. Last counted: Carriger v. Stewart, April 16, 1998. The recount excludes threshold, jurisdictional, procedural and summary actions, per curiams, separate writings and Section 10 explanations. The source is `tmp/chunk7_recount.py` operating on actual opinion tables, with the assignment-input tables as the reading handoff; earlier assignment totals are not authority.
Eligible authors: Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Lowest eligible count: 6. Balance pool: all eight eligible Associates, each at 6 or 7. Chosen author: Souter. His Hill tax-category analysis and Smiley treatment of statutory permission, agency classification and retained limits give concrete fit for explaining this transition without collapsing tax accounts or statutory authority into policy. Breyer is the strongest equal-count alternative given Regions' formula analysis; Souter's particular combination of tax-category and classification work better matches the insurer's claimed technical meaning. No accommodation is needed to secure a necessary Associate join, so expansion supplies no authorship preference. Stone's partial rationale support defeats complete-opinion eligibility; his office and interest cannot cure it. No opinion division for assignment purposes is necessary because Souter can carry all parts.

## 1998-04-21 ? Beach v. Ocwen Federal Bank, No. 97-5310.

Source: beach_v_ocwen_federal_bank_merits_1998-04-21.md

### Event

Beach v. Ocwen Federal Bank, No. 97-5310. Merits decision, April 21, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 7. Argued March 2, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Florida. The Beaches refinanced their home in 1986, stopped payments in 1991 and faced foreclosure in 1992. The trial court allowed damages offsets but rejected rescission on transaction-classification and expiration grounds. Florida's highest court addressed only expiration. The question is whether the Truth in Lending Act's federal rescission right survives its statutory expiration when asserted as a foreclosure defense.

### Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

### Assignment statements

Before the recount, the case calls for one signed opinion after plenary review, with a single complete statutory-duration holding, its express extension, the distinct defensive-damages clause and a confined affirmance. All nine support that judgment and every proposition; a split opinion or per curiam is unnecessary on the legal work. No form or proposition is chosen to alter counts.
The Chief assigns. Fresh actual-Record recount before this assignment: Stone-Zsela 5; Stevens 7; O'Connor 7; Scalia 6; Kennedy 7; Souter 7; Thomas 6; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 6. Counted opinions: 58. Last counted: Atlantic Mutual Insurance Co. v. Commissioner, April 21, 1998. The recount reads actual opinion tables, not prior assignment tallies, and excludes nonplenary/procedural/threshold matters, per curiams, separate writings and Section 10 explanations.
Eligible authors: all nine. Lowest count: 5. Balance pool: Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer. Chosen author: Stone-Zsela. His approved formulation directly separates the duration of a substantive right, the distinct damages remedy and the foreclosure procedural setting, while expressly retaining unpresented remedies. That is the precise work needed here. Scalia is the strongest alternative because his Reiter writing can explain why affirmative-action recoupment differs; Breyer's Brockamp and Bay Area work also offers close fit. Both can express the common rationale, but neither has a material advantage over Stone's complete formulation, and Stone has the lower count. This is fit-based self-assignment on the actual remedy distinction, not importance, office or subject-matter interest. No necessary join depends on an unresolved accommodation, so expansion adds no preference. Every Associate's final join fits the frozen common scope without a changed commitment or invented circulation history.

## 1998-04-22 ? California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc., No. 96-1400.

Source: california_v_deep_sea_research_inc_merits_1998-04-22.md

### Event

California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc., No. 96-1400. Plenary merits decision, April 22, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 7. Argued December 1, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit. DSR seeks title or salvage rights in the Brother Jonathan, which sank in 1865 and was located by DSR in 1994. California asserts ownership under the Abandoned Shipwreck Act and state law but has never possessed the wreck. DSR began this action in 1991, recovered artifacts, purchased some insurer subrogation interests and received custodial and exclusive-salvor orders; final title and distribution were deferred. The question is whether California's asserted ownership bars the federal in-rem admiralty action under the Eleventh Amendment.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–II and IV | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports both judgment components | Nonpossession immunity rule, binding in-rem adjudication, mixed disposition and bounded remand |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Governs independent ASA inquiry on remand | Maritime abandonment construction, statutory conditions and qualifications; no final title finding |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Joins the Court; additional explanation | Broader historical account of in-rem admiralty, without deciding a State-possessed case |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | Ginsburg and Breyer | Joins the Court; reservation | Does not treat the possession distinction as permanently resolving the broader constitutional question |

Stone-Zsela joins Parts I–II and IV and the source-specific remand, without joining Part III's additional maritime-abandonment construction.

### Assignment statements

Before counting, the legal work requires one signed opinion after plenary review. Parts I–II state the traditional immunity boundary and its binding in-rem consequence; Part III supplies the separate ASA source, conditions and construction; Part IV gives the mixed disposition and bounded remand. These are substantive distinctions, not count-driven divisions. All nine support each formal judgment component. All eight Associates join the full governing rationale; Stone's stated scope does not include every Part III proposition.
The Chief assigns. Fresh actual-Record recount before assignment: Stone-Zsela 6; Stevens 7; O'Connor 7; Scalia 6; Kennedy 7; Souter 7; Thomas 6; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 6. Counted opinions: 59. Last counted: Beach v. Ocwen Federal Bank, April 21, 1998. Campbell is a threshold standing decision and is excluded, as are the other procedural/threshold actions, summaries, per curiams, separate writings and Section 10 explanations. Actual opinion tables, including the earlier records in this Run, control rather than prior assignment totals.
Eligible complete-opinion authors: Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Minimum eligible count: 6. Pool: all eight, at 6 or 7. Chosen author: Scalia. His frozen position expressly distinguishes the surviving traditional maritime rule from his broader constitutional immunity position, and separately rejects treating jurisdiction as ownership or congressional abrogation. That formulation directly addresses the opinion's principal risk of collapsing different lawful routes. Breyer is the strongest equal-count alternative for preserving the individual property questions and avoiding administrability as an ownership premise; Scalia's explicit separation of the immunity domains better fits the lead holding while carrying the agreed title limits. Thomas is also fully eligible, without any capability exclusion. No additional agreement is needed from a conditional Justice, so expansion supplies no separate preference. Stone is not eligible for the complete opinion because his approved remand does not supply every Part III interpretation; division among authors is unnecessary because one eligible author carries all parts.

## 1998-04-22 ? Miller v. Albright, No. 96-1060.

Source: miller_v_albright_merits_1998-04-22.md

### Event

Miller v. Albright, No. 96-1060. Plenary merits decision, April 22, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 7. Argued November 4, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the D.C. Circuit. The question concerns unequal citizenship-transmission requirements for a child born abroad to a citizen father rather than a citizen mother.

Miller was born in the Philippines on June 20, 1970. Her November 1991 citizenship application was denied; she applied again after a July 1992 Texas decree established the biological and legal parent-child relationship with her citizen father. The State Department accepted his citizenship and sufficient physical presence to transmit citizenship. Paternity is undisputed, although no formal clear-and-convincing finding is reported. The denial expressly rested on §1409(a)(4). The father initially sued with her, was dismissed, did not appeal, and the action was transferred from Texas to the District of Columbia. The district court dismissed for lack of power to grant the requested citizenship; the court of appeals recognized standing but sustained the statutory distinction.

The amended §1409(a) separately requires clear-and-convincing proof of blood relationship; the father's United States nationality at birth; his written agreement, unless deceased, to support the person until eighteen; and, while the person is under eighteen, legitimation under residence or domicile law, his written sworn acknowledgment, or competent-court paternity adjudication. The last alternatives are disjunctive. Miller may elect the former subsection, which required legitimation before twenty-one and did not contain the later support promise. Her decree followed both age deadlines. The former route retains the separate §1401 transmission predicates; the earlier ten-year presence period, including five years after fourteen, differs from the amended five-year period, including two after fourteen. The agency accepted sufficient presence; no deficiency under either applicable version is found.

Section 1409(c) separately gives the citizen mother's nationality at birth to a person born abroad and out of wedlock after December 23, 1952 when the mother previously had continuous physical presence for one year in the United States or an outlying possession. The constitutionality of the differing presence periods and every independent support application are outside this decision. No amended support or blood-proof requirement is added to the former-law route.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Affirms on narrow merits | Accepts father-right permission; sustains timely recognition under heightened scrutiny; does not adopt the other affirmance grounds |
| Concurrence in the judgment | O'Connor | Kennedy | Affirms on distinct claimant and own-claim grounds | Rejects father-right permission; applies rational review to the daughter's own claim; gives a contrary conditional assessment under heightened scrutiny |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Affirms on the requested citizenship remedy | Accepts father-right permission under existing precedent; does not decide the sex classification; rejects judicial enlargement of the statutory citizenship grant |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Souter and Breyer | Would reverse with directed declaratory recognition | Own equality claim and alternative father-right permission; actual-fit and historical-stereotype analysis; former-route constitutional correction and directed remedy |
| Dissent | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would reverse with a conditional remedial remand | Own equality claim; equivalent objective procedures; lawful remedial alternatives and conditional recognition |

No opinion commands a majority. Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer expressly agree on the limited father-right permission stated below through their respective writings; that agreement does not join them to one another's merits or remedy analyses.

### Assignment statements

Before counting or choosing authors, the five affirmance votes support three independent rationales: Stevens's merits ground; O'Connor/Kennedy's third-party refusal and rational review of the own claim; Scalia/Thomas's citizenship-remedy ground. The legal form is therefore one signed opinion announcing the judgment, two separate concurrences in the judgment, a combined three-Associate dissent covering threshold, fit, statutory history and directed remedy, and Stone's distinct conditional-remedy dissent. No majority opinion is invented. The shared father-right permission is stated as an independently majority-supported proposition across express positions, without converting it into joins in the lead's other reasoning.
Fresh actual-Record recount before assignment: Stone-Zsela 6; Stevens 7; O'Connor 7; Scalia 7; Kennedy 7; Souter 7; Thomas 6; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 6. Counted signed plenary lead opinions: 60. Last counted: California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc., April 22, 1998. Campbell is threshold-only and excluded; procedural/threshold rulings, per curiams, summaries, separate writings and Section 10 explanations remain excluded. Actual opinion tables, not earlier assignment tallies, control.
The judgment coalition is Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. The Chief is outside it; Stevens, the senior participating Associate in it, assigns. The Chief's minimum-plus-one balance pool is therefore inapplicable. No member of this coalition supports all three rationales; complete eligibility for the chosen narrow merits lead consists of Stevens alone. O'Connor and Kennedy can write their distinct standing/own-claim concurrence; Scalia and Thomas can write their distinct remedial concurrence. These are separate grounds, not parts divided to manipulate counts. Stevens assigns the opinion announcing the judgment to himself because his supported approach answers the properly presented recognition condition on its merits while preserving the refusal to decide broader deference or remedial questions. His date-eligible Virginia, Kelley and Klinger commitments support genuine-objective review, and his frozen position chooses a substantive legal answer over a new universal citizenship-immunity rule. Seniority supplies assignment authority, not a presumption of majority support for his rationale. Stone is ineligible for this affirmance lead and has no self-assignment claim.

## 1998-04-27 ? Chaudhuri v. Tennessee; Sixth Circuit No. 96-5538; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

Source: chaudhuri_v_tennessee_merits_1998-04-27.md

### Event

Chaudhuri v. Tennessee, Sixth Circuit No. 96-5538. Decided April 27, 1998, on review of the Sixth Circuit’s judgment at 130 F.3d 232. The questions concern officially arranged, nonsectarian invocations and benedictions at public-university functions, including a claim of practical employment pressure, and officially designated moments of silence adopted in their place.

Tennessee State University formerly invited clergy to deliver prayers at commencement and other university functions, requested nonsectarian wording, and incorporated the prayers into its programs. It replaced spoken prayers with silence in 1993. Chaudhuri, a tenured professor, sought damages and declaratory and injunctive relief. The lower court decided the former-prayer merits because the damages claim remained live; it upheld both practices. The live damages controversy permits review without deciding whether any particular defendant is ultimately liable.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join Part I only | Supports partial affirmance, reversal and remand | Part I: review scope, live damages controversy and genuine silence; Part II: official-prayer rule and prayer remand |
| Dissent in part, Part I | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Would affirm the former-prayer ruling; joins the Court’s Part I | Voluntary adult ceremonial relationship |
| Dissent in part, Part II | Scalia | Thomas | Same disposition | Distinct compulsion account |
| Separate dissent in part | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would affirm the former-prayer ruling; joins the Court’s Part I and Part I of Scalia’s dissent | Practical-pressure analysis; does not make formal punishment indispensable |

### Assignment statements

**Form decided before counting:** One signed merits opinion, with a common threshold-and-silence part and a six-Justice official-prayer part. Both questions arise from the same university program and lower judgment; the different joins require parts, not different lead authors. The opinion applies the updated Lemon inquiry, permits genuine silence, and applies the official-worship prohibition to the university’s arranged prayers. Ordinary remand preserves independent liability and relief questions.
**Assignment authority:** The Chief Justice is in the tentative judgment coalition and assigns. Agreement on the entire proposed rationale, rather than judgment alone, establishes eligibility.
| Justice | Count before assignment |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 6 |
| Stevens | 8 |
| O'Connor | 7 |
| Scalia | 7 |
| Kennedy | 7 |
| Souter | 7 |
| Thomas | 6 |
| Ginsburg | 7 |
| Breyer | 6 |
**Last event counted:** Miller v. Albright, No. 96-1060.; 1998-04-22.
**Eligible authors:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.
**Balance pool:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer; eligible minimum 6, ceiling 7.
**Author selected:** Breyer. Fit governs because the frozen coalition already shares the complete functional distinction and no necessary join requires new accommodation. Breyer can state the concrete difference between nondevotional silence and officially organized prayer while keeping the supported record and remedial reservations separate. His Sherman, Ingebretsen and Tanford joins support precisely that task. O'Connor brings her Sherman authorship but has the higher count. Stone is the strongest equal-count alternative through his Tanford opinion, yet extending its settled official-worship boundary and deciding this actual silence practice requires no new formulation he uniquely supplies. Breyer can carry that objective equally well; there is no concrete self-assignment advantage. The dissent authorship follows the recorded distinct grounds and does not enter the balance count.

## 1998-04-28 ? Edwards v. United States; No. 96-8732.

Source: edwards_v_united_states_merits_1998-04-28.md

### Event

Edwards v. United States, No. 96-8732. Argued February 23, 1998; decided April 28, 1998. On review of the Seventh Circuit’s criminal sentencing judgment. The question is whether a general conspiracy verdict following an instruction referring to cocaine or cocaine base prevents the judge from determining the drugs properly attributable for Guidelines sentencing.

The indictment charged both cocaine and crack. The jury returned a general guilty verdict; the judge independently found that each petitioner’s conduct involved both drugs. The transactions were interrelated, and petitioners did not contend that the crack activity fell outside the same course of conduct or common plan. Each sentence remained within the cocaine-only statutory limits given the cocaine quantities attributed to that petitioner.

## 1998-04-29 ? Calderon v. Thompson; No. 97-215.

Source: calderon_v_thompson_merits_1998-04-29.md

### Event

Calderon v. Thompson, No. 97-215. Argued December 9, 1997; decided April 29, 1998. On review of the Ninth Circuit’s August 3, 1997 recall of its mandate in Thompson’s first federal habeas proceeding. California seeks reversal and reinstatement of the June 11 mandate denying relief.

Thompson was convicted in 1983 of murder and rape; the rape special circumstance supported death eligibility. His January 1990 federal application produced rape-related relief and vacation of the death sentence in 1995, while the murder claim was denied. A circuit panel reversed the granted relief. After January 17, 1997 notice concerning rejection of the en banc suggestion, a March 12 request for belated consideration described a law-clerk communication failure; another judge supported it, and the panel author rejected it. The mandate issued June 11.

The en banc majority later reported a separate court-initiated recall request shortly after issuance. That initiative preceded the prisoner’s July new-evidence motion but was not an entered recall or stay. The August 3 recall, after the circuit’s stated wait for state proceedings, rested on the first application’s claims and evidence and excluded the later Leitch account. It restored conditional rape-conviction and rape-special-circumstance relief and the associated death-sentence vacation, directed further murder review, and vacated the then-entered execution order.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Would affirm the recall | Exceptional institutional correction; no independent innocence or ineffective-assistance holding |

### Assignment statements

**Form decided before counting:** One signed plenary opinion: a unanimous channel-and-temporal-law part, followed by the five-Justice completed-merits recall rule and distinct crime/eligibility gateway applications. A substantive dissent explains the four-Justice exceptional institutional-repair ground. The two gateways concern different components of the same recall and require separate legal statements, not separate lead authors.
**Assignment authority:** The Chief Justice is in the tentative judgment coalition and assigns. Agreement on the entire proposed rationale, rather than judgment alone, establishes eligibility.

**Author selected:** O'Connor. Fit governs because the five-Justice ground is settled and no necessary join depends on a new accommodation. The selected author brings her Felker opinion separating habeas channels and her Carriger dissent evaluating a complete gateway record, the two concrete tasks this opinion must coordinate. Kennedy offers strong eligibility-gateway fit through Sawyer, but the chosen author can state that preserved rule while integrating the first-application channel and the corrected chronology. ## 1998-04-29 ? United States v. Estate of Romani; No. 96-1613.

Source: united_states_v_estate_of_romani_merits_1998-04-29.md

### Event

United States v. Estate of Romani, No. 96-1613. Argued January 12, 1998; decided April 29, 1998. On review of the Pennsylvania Supreme Court’s judgment giving an established judgment lien priority over later federal tax liens in an insolvent decedent’s estate.

A $400,000 judgment lien attached to Romani’s existing Cambria County land before the federal notices. The later tax liens totaled approximately $490,000. At his death the estate’s sole asset was the land, worth $53,001. The administrator obtained authority to transfer it to the judgment creditor in lieu of execution. The United States objected under 31 U.S.C. §3713 despite acknowledging that its tax liens were invalid against this judgment lien under 26 U.S.C. §6323(a). The question is whether the general federal priority defeats that specific protection.

## 1998-05-04 ? Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department; Ninth Circuit No. 96-55545; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

Source: perry_v_los_angeles_police_department_merits_1998-05-04.md

### Event

Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department, Ninth Circuit No. 96-55545. Decided May 4, 1998, on review of the Ninth Circuit’s judgment at 121 F.3d 1365. The questions concern an ordinance permitting nonprofit organizations, but not unaffiliated individual speakers, to solicit donations and sell qualifying message-bearing merchandise on the Venice Boardwalk, and whether that distinction satisfies public-forum tailoring and alternative-channel requirements.

Section 42.15 prohibits vending goods and soliciting alms or donations on the designated oceanfront public ways. One exception permits newspapers, magazines, periodicals and printed matter commonly sold or distributed by news vendors. The separate nonprofit exception permits nonprofit donation solicitation and nonprofit merchandise carrying a religious, philosophical or ideological message relevant to the organization’s purpose.

Perry performed Sikh religious music and sold his recordings; Newman solicited and sold message-bearing material for an unincorporated animal-rights group. Both were cited for lacking nonprofit documentation. The trial court found the goods inseparably connected to protected messages but entered judgment against them under Rule 52(c). The Ninth Circuit reversed. Los Angeles seeks restoration of the trial judgment.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the judgment | Entire opinion |

### Assignment statements

**Form decided before counting:** One signed plenary merits opinion applying ordinary public-forum tailoring while assuming content neutrality. The concrete nonprofit-status defect resolves the challenge; content classification, independent alternative-channel adequacy and severability are not separately decided. The shared rationale needs neither a separate writing nor divided authorship.
**Assignment authority:** The Chief Justice is in the tentative judgment coalition and assigns. Agreement on the entire proposed rationale, rather than judgment alone, establishes eligibility.

| Breyer | 7 |

**Author selected:** Stevens. Fit governs because all nine share the actual tailoring ground without an unresolved formulation objection. Stevens wrote Discovery Network, which supplies the exact relation-between-classification-and-harm analysis, and Ladue, which supplies the assumed-neutrality method without an unnecessary content ruling. Those are concrete authoring advantages for explaining why nonprofit paperwork does not change these speakers physical conduct while preserving ordinary forum management and declining a new alternatives holding. ## 1998-05-04 ? Ricci v. Village of Arlington Heights; No. 97-501.

Source: ricci_v_village_of_arlington_heights_merits_1998-05-04.md

### Event

Ricci v. Village of Arlington Heights, No. 97-501. Argued April 21, 1998; decided May 4, 1998. On review of the Seventh Circuit’s affirmance of summary judgment, 116 F.3d 288. The question concerns the reasonableness of automatic station-house custody for operating a business without the required license, accepting probable cause and state and local arrest authority.

Officers at Ricci’s telemarketing business asked about his license, and he acknowledged having none. Under Village policy they took him to the station, locked him in an interview room for approximately an hour while preparing enforcement documents, and released him on recognizance. His wife obtained the license, and the charge was dismissed. The civil licensing ordinance carried a $5–$500 fine for each offense, with each day a separate offense; no accumulated fine is adjudicated here.

The separate premises-search claim was settled, and dismissal of the no-probable-cause claim was not appealed. A distinct warrant-clause argument first raised at appellate oral argument was held waived; Ricci conceded an arrest under a warrant would have been reasonable. The preserved question concerns the objective extent of this custody, not a revived warrant, search or probable-cause claim.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Breyer, Stone-Zsela | Targeted reversal and remand | Additional-restraint rule; rejection of categorical probable-cause/local-authority ground; setting aside adverse custody summary judgment |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Part II | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Breyer | Same reversal and remand; leaves actual application for the lower courts | Four-Justice explanation of the unresolved enforcement-connection inquiry and remand scope |
| Concurrence in Part I and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand; would decide actual custody unreasonable | Objective mismatch on this record; remand limited to remaining liability, defenses and relief; does not join Part II’s application reservation |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy and Souter | Would affirm | This continuing violation and short enforcement processing satisfy the bounded reasonableness inquiry |

### Assignment statements

**Form decided before counting:** One signed opinion announcing the judgment, with Part I an Opinion of the Court on the added-restraint rule and targeted reversal, and Part II a four-Justice explanation preserving the actual application for remand. Stone joins Part I and the reversal/remand judgment but separately concludes the custody was unreasonable and would confine remand to remaining liability and relief. The four affirmers dissent on the sufficiency of the actual enforcement connection. The genuine application difference determines the parts before any count; no Marks synthesis or division of lead authors is warranted because four Justices can carry the entire lead rationale.

Breyer offers equally practical record assessment, while the higher-count author of Robinette offers strong rule-construction capability. ## 1998-05-07 ? Benjamin v. Jacobson; Second Circuit No. 96-7957, 124 F.3d 162. No separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

Source: benjamin_v_jacobson_merits_1998-05-07.md

### Event

Benjamin v. Jacobson, Second Circuit No. 96-7957, 124 F.3d 162. Decided May 7, 1998, on review of the Second Circuit's judgment concerning the termination of prospective relief in New York City jail consent decrees. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

The questions are whether §3626(b) validly changes the conditions for continuing relief, what it terminates, and how the preservation inquiry must proceed. The district court vacated the decrees and declined to consider current evidence beyond the existing record. The Court of Appeals upheld the statute, rejected vacatur, treated the decree duties as surviving for state enforcement, and required an evidentiary opportunity. Only the termination framework and the presented constitutional objections are before the Court.

## 1998-05-11 ? Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10; Second Circuit No. 96-9633, 127 F.3d 207. No separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

Source: bronx_household_of_faith_v_community_school_district_no_10_merits_1998-05-11.md

### Event

Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10, Second Circuit No. 96-9633, 127 F.3d 207. Decided May 11, 1998, on review of the judgment sustaining the school district's worship and religious-instruction exclusions. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

The church sought to rent the M.S. 206B gymnasium each Sunday for services including hymns, communion, Bible reading, preaching and teaching. The school serves grades 5–7. The district opened facilities for educational, civic, social, recreational and community-welfare activities, including secular ethical and character instruction; it had rented to the church for sports and a banquet. Its policy barred religious services and instruction but permitted religious discussion and viewpoints. The stipulated use record covers one school year and establishes consistent exclusion of worship services. The questions concern viewpoint discrimination and the proposed establishment justification; the separate free-exercise, equality and RFRA rulings are outside review.

## 1998-05-18 ? Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, No. 96-779.

Source: arkansas_educational_television_commission_v_forbes_merits_1998-05-18.md

### Event

Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, No. 96-779. Argued October 8, 1997; decided May 18, 1998. On review of the Eighth Circuit's judgment, 93 F.3d 497, reversing judgment for the Commission on Forbes's First Amendment challenge to exclusion from a televised candidate debate.

The state broadcaster planned a selective, moderated debate program. Its spring 1992 selection category covered major-party candidates and other candidates with strong popular support. Forbes later qualified for the ballot, requested inclusion and was denied. Staff cited lack of campaign organization, appreciable voter support and press recognition as a serious candidate. The jury found that political pressure and disagreement with his views did not cause the exclusion. The question is whether this actual program created a forum requiring his admission and whether the exclusion was constitutionally permissible.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and the specified judgment | Entire opinion. |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Would sustain the appellate judgment against the exclusion | Insufficiently definite government candidate-selection standards, without universal ballot access. |

### Assignment statements

**Form decided before counting:** One signed plenary opinion on forum classification and the actual exclusion; one dissent explaining the standards objection. No division or added rule is needed.
**Assignment authority:** The Chief belongs to the six-Justice judgment coalition and assigns.
| Justice | Count before assignment |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 7 |
| Stevens | 9 |
| O'Connor | 8 |
| Scalia | 8 |
| Kennedy | 9 |
| Souter | 8 |
| Thomas | 7 |
| Ginsburg | 8 |
| Breyer | 8 |
**Last event counted:** Rice v. Paladin Enterprises, May 14, 1998. Lewis's subsequent clerk dismissal carries no credit. The fresh recount uses actual opinion tables. Forbes is alphabetically first in the independent May 18 group.
**Eligible authors:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer.
**Balance pool:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer; minimum 7, ceiling 8. Kennedy's count of 9 excludes him from this assignment despite his case-specific expertise.
**Author selected:** O'Connor. Fit governs the settled coalition: her Cornelius opinion directly supplies the selective-access classification and its neutrality constraint, the central legal work here. Stone and Thomas have lower counts and can carry the rule, but O'Connor's direct formulation of the governing distinction offers the concrete advantage. Stone's remedial and no-new-publication limits are fully shared, without an accommodation requiring self-assignment. No fictional revision or changed vote is used.

## 1998-05-18 ? Bousley v. United States, No. 96-8516.

Source: bousley_v_united_states_merits_1998-05-18.md

### Event

Bousley v. United States, No. 96-8516. Argued March 3, 1998; decided May 18, 1998. On certiorari to the Eighth Circuit's denial of relief in a first §2255 proceeding.

Bousley pleaded guilty to a drug offense and using a firearm during and in relation to drug trafficking. He appealed his sentence without challenging the plea's validity. After Bailey explained that use requires active employment, he challenged the understanding of the firearm charge underlying his plea. The questions are whether that claim is available on collateral review, how procedural default affects it, and what factual innocence requires. The indictment charged use, not carrying.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer throughout; Stone-Zsela in I–III and IV.B; Stevens in I | I: informed plea and substantive meaning; II: default; III: factual gateway; IV.A: actually forgone more-serious charges; IV.B: use-only indictment and conditional remand |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | None | Joins I; rejects the default/innocence prerequisites and seeks immediate vacatur with permission to plead anew |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm because cause is absent and the trial-conviction innocence gateway should not extend to this plea |

### Assignment statements

Form first: one signed plenary lead opinion, with I addressing informed plea/substantive law, II default, III the factual gateway, IV.A actual forgone more-serious offenses and IV.B this indictment/remand. Separate writings preserve Stevens's distinct remedy and the Scalia/Thomas gateway objection. The parts follow legal differences before counting.
Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns from the conditional-remand coalition. Fresh actual-opinion-table counts immediately before assignment: Stone-Zsela 7; Stevens 9; O'Connor 9; Scalia 8; Kennedy 9; Souter 8; Thomas 7; Ginsburg 8; Breyer 8. Last counted event: Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, May 18, 1998. Eligible complete-opinion authors: O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Minimum 8; pool includes all five at 8 or 9. Stone's partial rationale join does not qualify him for the entire opinion; no split authorship is needed because five can carry every part.
Ginsburg is selected for fit. Her Lindh opinion separates statutory channels and operative provisions, the central task in coordinating substantive innocence, default, plea validity and a limited mandate. O'Connor's Bailey authorship makes her a strong alternative, but Ginsburg can preserve that settled construction equally while carrying the distinct procedural stages and has the lower count. Souter and Breyer are equally counted alternatives; Ginsburg's provision-specific formulation supplies the concrete fit. No expansion accommodation or commitment change is invented.

Montana v. Crow Tribe of Indians, No. 96-1829. Argued January 14, 1998; decided May 18, 1998. On certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, 92 F.3d 826, which required transfer to the Tribe of the entire disputed state tax collection from Westmoreland.

The state taxes on coal from the Tribe's ceded-strip minerals were held invalid. The present dispute concerns approximately $46.8 million in severance taxes collected during 1975–1982 and $11.4 million in gross-proceeds taxes during 1975–1987, before the respective registry deposits. The question is whether the established invalidity requires payment of all those receipts to the Tribe, rather than an inquiry into its entitlement and the proper equitable remedy.

Both can state the common rule, and Stone has the lower count. Thomas is equally counted and fully capable, but Stone's express treatment of taxpayer, fund, agreements and the anti-windfall/anti-retention limits supplies the case-specific advantage. This is fit and faithful scope coordination, not an authorship claim based on office or importance. Souter writes the concurrence joined by O'Connor.

On writ of certiorari under §1259 to the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces, which granted the marital-privacy question, specified the closed-session question, and affirmed on December 16, 1997.

The questions are whether marital privacy bars this Article 125 conviction on the proved assault record, and whether the military judge's closed-session contact violated Article 39(b) and materially prejudiced substantial rights. Thompson seeks relief on both; the United States seeks affirmance. The court-martial also convicted him of assault and aggravated assault. Its approved sentence was a bad-conduct discharge, thirty months' confinement, total forfeitures, and reduction to the lowest enlisted grade.

### Opinion Topology

Thomas delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by every other participating Justice. All nine support both controlling holdings, their qualifications, and the affirmance. There are no separate writings.

### Assignment statements

One signed lead opinion with two holdings is appropriate before any counts: the plenary review resolves distinct constitutional and statutory questions through shared, bounded reasoning. No separate writing or split authorship is needed. All nine join each holding and the entire judgment; each is eligible on substantive grounds. Stone assigns as Chief from the judgment coalition. Direct majority authority controls, with no Marks aggregation.

Branstad, May 21, 1998, preceding this independent peer alphabetically. All nine are eligible for the complete opinion. Minimum 7; balance pool: Stone-Zsela, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. Thomas writes for fit and balance. His Weiss concurrence keeps the military system's enacted protections operative, and his Sullivan join distinguishes an assessable recorded violation from the absence of a valid verdict. Those premises carry the statutory holding without replacing the Government's burden with defense silence. His frozen privacy position rests only on the missing voluntary-intimacy premise, allowing the narrow assault holding without resolving consensual conduct. Souter's contextual Weiss/Casey positions are a strong alternative and support the same two limits; both can express the complete shared rule, and Thomas has the lower count. Stone's approved two-part formulation is likewise faithfully achievable by Thomas at the lower count, so self-assignment adds no coalition or scope advantage. No expansion accommodation, new join, hypothetical constitutional ruling or invented circulation history is used. This signed plenary merits opinion receives one lead-author credit for Thomas.

Air Line Pilots Association v. Miller, No. 97-428. Plenary merits decision, May 26, 1998. Argued March 23, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, 108 F.3d 1415. The question is whether nonmember employees who have not agreed to arbitrate must exhaust a union's impartial arbitration procedure before bringing their compulsory-fee objections to federal court. Review concerns exhaustion for the 1992 fee-calculation dispute. Earlier facial challenges to the agency-shop clause were resolved against the pilots and were not appealed; they and other fee years are outside this review. The district court required exhaustion; the court of appeals rejected that requirement and remanded the remaining 1992 fee disputes.

### Opinion Topology

### Assignment statements

Before counting, the legal form is one signed opinion after plenary merits review, deciding the single exhaustion question. Its governing propositions are the absence of this compulsory private-arbitration prerequisite, the distinction from statutory and appropriate administrative exhaustion, and the preservation of independent merits adjudication and ordinary litigation management. A signed opinion explains the disputed relation between Hudson, contractual assent and judicial exhaustion; no split of propositions or cases is needed.

Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) concerns the Sixth Circuit's November 5, 1997 Commerce Clause determinations against compulsory routing to Metro's waste-to-energy facility and the separate fee on disposal outside its system. The court of appeals exercised §1292(a)(1) jurisdiction over the injunction rulings, reversing denial of the routing injunction and affirming the fee injunction. Metro owns the facility; Nashville Thermal Transfer Corporation operates it. Residential routing permits the Director's specific written authorization for another destination. Nonresidential routing is limited to allocations needed for the reported 6,300-ton weekly requirement, based on monthly reporting, at-least-quarterly pro rata calculations using the best available data, and advance facility and collector notices. The separate charge is $2 per cubic yard or $8 per ton on the specified waste generated or collected within Metro and disposed at non-Metro facilities inside or outside Metro; NTTC and other Metro-owned sites are exempt.

United States v. Lacy; Ninth Circuit No. 95-30370, 119 F.3d 742. Plenary merits decision, May 28, 1998, on submission. No separate Supreme Court argument date is established. On review of the Ninth Circuit's July 10, 1997 affirmance of the possession conviction after a jury trial. ## 1998-06-01 ? City of Florence v. Chipman / Stemler v. City of Florence; Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-5993 and 96-5996, 126 F.3d 856; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

### Event

City of Florence v. Chipman / Stemler v. City of Florence; Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-5993 and 96-5996, 126 F.3d 856. Plenary merits decision, June 1, 1998, on submission. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established. Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) concerns the Sixth Circuit's October 8, 1997 reinstatement of Black's individual substantive-due-process claim and Stemler's individual equal-protection claim; rehearing was denied November 13, 1997. The plaintiffs appealed final individual dismissals and municipal summary judgments. This is not an interlocutory officer appeal. The questions concern compelled dangerous placement, purposeful discriminatory selection despite probable cause, and qualified immunity for conduct on February 18–19, 1994.

### Opinion Topology

O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine adopt the narrow restraint, arbitrary-selection and conduct-time immunity holdings with their individual-proof limits. Scalia files a concurrence, joined by Thomas, preserving their direct-restraint basis and rejecting any implication that they adopt a broader conscience framework. Their concurrence withholds assent from no proposition actually adopted by the Court.

### Assignment statements

Before the recount or author choice, the legal form is one signed opinion after full authorized merits submission. It decides both individual claims and their conduct-time immunity issues on narrow existing-law grounds. On Black, the Court adopts direct restraint and personally known dangerous transfer, with actual awareness, authority, reasonable opportunity, unreasonable disregard and causation. It does not adopt a broader free-standing conscience framework or transport the entire Eighth Amendment prison framework to roadside conduct. On Stemler, it adopts the old prohibition against purposeful arbitrary group-based selection with actual comparison and discriminatory effect. All nine can join these complete propositions and the limits; no section is added or removed to affect eligibility.
The Chief assigns. Fresh recount from the actual prior opinion tables and all eight earlier new Records yields **Stone-Zsela 9; Stevens 9; O'Connor 9; Scalia 9; Kennedy 9; Souter 10; Thomas 9; Ginsburg 10; Breyer 9**. **Last counted event:** United States v. Lacy, May 28, 1998. **Eligible authors:** all nine, each supporting the complete narrow opinion independently of fallback. **Minimum:** 9. **Balance pool:** all nine eligible Justices, each at 9 or 10. **Assigned author:** O'Connor, for fit. Her actual Pinder opinion distinguishes a noncoercive assurance from affirmative restraint and separates an unresolved constitutional extension from conduct-time immunity. Florence requires precisely that distinction while preserving Pinder's earlier result and individually testing actual coercion, information and response. Her Farmer and Lewis joins support the actual-awareness and opportunity limits without importing a pursuit rule. Stone's equal count and approved custody/notice separation make him the strongest self-assignment alternative; O'Connor offers the concrete advantage of expressing the operative boundary of her own Pinder rationale across this different custodial record. ### Event

Reeves was convicted of two Nebraska felony murders arising from sexual assault or attempted sexual assault. The trial court refused instructions on second-degree murder and manslaughter because Nebraska does not classify them as lesser included offenses of felony murder. A three-judge panel later imposed death. The Eighth Circuit rejected a separate due-process ground but granted conditional habeas relief on the instruction theory. ## 1998-06-08 ? National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt; D.C. Circuit No. 96-5354; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

Source: national_association_of_home_builders_v_babbitt_merits_1998-06-08.md

### Event

National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt; D.C. Circuit No. 96-5354. Plenary merits decision, June 8, 1998. On review of the D.C. Circuit's December 5, 1997 judgment, 130 F.3d 1041, affirming summary judgment sustaining application of ESA section 9(a)(1)(B) to the challenged development projects affecting the Delhi Sands flower-loving fly. The question is whether Congress may prohibit the intrastate taking of an endangered species confined to one State and not traded, based on asserted aggregate effects on interstate commerce. The reviewed applications involve hospital, road and commercial development. The builders seek reversal and declaratory relief against those applications. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing or part | Author | Joined by | Scope and relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Enacted taking class and rejection of the economic-subset ground; five Justices. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II-A | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Reasonably necessary anti-evasion/material-impairment route; seven Justices. |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts II-B and III | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Independent substantial-interstate-externality route; the Government's material-support responsibility, independent eligibility review and legislative latitude; five Justices for each part. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Vacatur/remand and preserved statutory limits; five Justices. |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | O'Connor | Joins the complete Court opinion and judgment; explains the concrete interstate boundary and territorial-control requirement. |
| Opinion concurring in Parts II, III and IV and the judgment, and dissenting from Part I | Breyer | Ginsburg | Retains the economic-application objection to Part I, but affirmatively joins both routes, the support/deference rule and remand as the proper consequence of the Court's independently controlling class ruling. |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter | Would affirm on the economic-habitat-conversion ground; joins no part of the Court opinion. |
| Opinion concurring in Parts I and II-A, and dissenting from Parts II-B, III and IV and the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Joins the class ruling and regime route; rejects the independent externality route and would reverse and remand for confined relief. |

### Assignment statements

Form first: one signed Opinion of the Court resolves the enacted-class question, the two interstate routes, evidentiary responsibility and the consequence for this constitutional appeal. The distinct disagreement over affirmance and the disagreement over the independent externality route require separate dissents. Kennedy and O'Connor explain the territorial limitation while joining the complete opinion. Breyer and Ginsburg retain their economic-application objection but join the remaining parts and the judgment. The legal divisions identify the enacted class, regime necessity, independent externality, evidentiary responsibility and remedy; they are not chosen to influence any count. This form and the scope of the governing propositions follow the legal issues, independently of authorship counts.
Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns from the tentative judgment majority. Immediately before this assignment, 91 qualifying signed lead opinions count: Stone-Zsela 10; Stevens 10; O'Connor 10; Scalia 10; Kennedy 10; Souter 10; Thomas 10; Ginsburg 10; Breyer 11. The last counted event is Muscarello/Cleveland, June 8, 1998, authored by Breyer. The count uses the current term's signed plenary lead-opinion topologies in effective-date and same-date alphabetical order. Per curiam opinions, procedural actions and separate writings do not count.
The only eligible author for the complete opinion is Stone-Zsela: his approved position joins every governing proposition and the full disposition. The frozen reconciled commitments of O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas rest on Path B alone and adopt no externality route, and those of Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer affirm on Path A; O'Connor's and Kennedy's full joins, and Ginsburg's and Breyer's joins in Parts II-IV, come from final compatibility. Stone-Zsela, at 10, is therefore the only balance-pool member. Stone-Zsela writes. Expansion is the assignment task: the formulation must retain O'Connor and Kennedy's enacted-class and enforceable-boundary requirements while enabling Ginsburg and Breyer to accept the doctrinal and remedial consequences of Part I without surrendering their own economic-application position. The two supported routes, legislative latitude, absence of a numerical or heightened-proof rule, and existing-record mandate supply the necessary agreement on Parts II-IV. The legal parts remain independently justified questions; no proposition is divided for assignment purposes.
Stone's position supplies that precise two-route formulation and coordinated mandate, and his authorship preserves the respective propositions and limited joins. Kennedy's actual-operation opinions and O'Connor's regime analysis are directly relevant to that formulation, but neither frozen commitment joins the independent externality route, supported-record requirement or nonfinal remand. Self-assignment rests on concrete coalition-formulation work, not the subject's importance or the Chief's office. The lead counts once for Stone; no separate writing counts.

| Opinion of the Court, Part IV | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Vacatur/remand and preserved statutory limits; five Justices. |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | O'Connor | Joins the complete Court opinion and judgment; explains the concrete interstate boundary and territorial-control requirement. |

The prosecution presented straw purchases in Ohio, purchasers' false statements, assurances about serial-number removal and Brooklyn street sales; it presented no evidence of Bryan's awareness of the licensing requirement. ## 1998-06-15 ? Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation; No. 96-1578.

Source: phillips_v_washington_legal_foundation_merits_1998-06-15.md

### Event

Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation, No. 96-1578. Argued January 13, 1998; decided June 15, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Fifth Circuit. The question is whether interest actually generated by client funds in a mandatory IOLTA account is the private property of the principal’s owner for Takings Clause purposes.

Texas requires lawyers to place client funds in IOLTA when they cannot reasonably be expected to earn interest for the client, or when expected interest would not cover the costs of establishing and maintaining the account, service charges, accounting and tax reporting. The interest goes to the Texas Equal Access to Justice Foundation to finance legal services for persons with low incomes. The District Court granted summary judgment on the ground that clients had no property interest in the proceeds. The Fifth Circuit reversed that determination and left the remaining takings questions for further proceedings.

### Event

Caron v. United States, No. 97-6270. Argued April 21, 1998; decided June 22, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the First Circuit. The question is whether §921(a)(20) preserves a conviction's federal firearms consequences when state restoration of civil rights retains a restriction on some firearm possession.

Massachusetts restored Caron's civil rights by operation of law. He held the required identification permit and could possess rifles and shotguns because the convictions were more than five years old, but could not possess handguns outside his home or business. A December 1993 search recovered six rifles and shotguns and ammunition. A jury returned four §922(g)(1) convictions. The initial enhancement counted one California and three Massachusetts convictions. After an en banc decision rejected the requirement of individualized restoration, the District Court excluded the Massachusetts convictions; the reviewed First Circuit judgment counted them because a significant firearms restriction remained.

## 1998-06-22 ? Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District, No. 96-1866.

Source: gebser_v_lago_vista_independent_school_district_merits_1998-06-22.md

### Event

Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District, No. 96-1866. Argued March 25, 1998; decided June 22, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Fifth Circuit. The question is what showing permits private damages against a school district under Title IX for a teacher's sexual harassment of a student. Review concerns only the appealed Title IX judgment against the district.

In October 1992, parents of two other students complained to the principal about Waldrop's classroom comments. The principal met with him, received an apology and assurance against repetition, cautioned him and informed the guidance counselor, but did not transmit the complaint to the superintendent, the Title IX coordinator. Gebser did not report the relationship herself. Police discovered it in January 1993, and the district terminated Waldrop.

The evidence about grievance procedures is disputed. The superintendent's affidavit described a policy, while deposition testimony described no formal policy; she did not remember whether a handbook notifying students of procedures had been distributed. Gebser testified that she probably would have reported had she known what to do, while describing bewilderment and fear. These are evidentiary accounts, not findings of institutional fault or causation. The District Court granted summary judgment for the district, and the Fifth Circuit affirmed under an actual-supervisory-knowledge requirement.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|

| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Joins Part I and supports reversal/remand; states independent actual-authority/material-aid and negligent-correction grounds. |
| Separate concurrence | Ginsburg | Souter, Breyer | Proposes the limited avoidable-harm defense within their broader framework; Stevens reserves it and Stone-Zsela leaves defenses unresolved. |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm under Kinman's exclusive actual-notice rule and its record-specific insufficiency application. |

Breyer's and Ginsburg's separate authorship serves their respective supported grounds and adds no lead count. O'Connor states the four dissenters' shared threshold and evidence position. One lead opinion, counted once for Stone-Zsela, results.
No material new non-Stone rationale was circulated. The additive no-Stone clarification lawfully establishes the specific formal remedy; Part I's direct agreement is already express, and Part II's Marks force is a legal authority determination rather than an invented join. All immutable freeze bytes remain intact.

## 1998-06-22 ? Pearson v. Hines; Ninth Circuit Nos. 95-17259 and 95-17260; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

Source: pearson_v_hines_merits_1998-06-22.md

### Event

Pearson v. Hines; Ninth Circuit Nos. 95-17259 and 95-17260, 108 F.3d 265. Decided June 22, 1998, on review of the Ninth Circuit’s affirmance of the judgment against Pearson. The questions concern a knowingly false disciplinary accusation retaliating for a prisoner’s lawful grievance, the effect of Superintendent v. Hill’s some-evidence rule, and the asserted need for an additional injury or actual chilling.

The conduct occurred January 19, 1992. Evidence included Hines’s prior grievances, guards’ descriptions of him as a frequent complainer and his statement that morning that he would file a grievance against Pearson. Pearson did not challenge the jury’s finding that his accusation was false. The discipline imposed ten days’ confinement and loss of television. The jury awarded $100 compensatory and $1,000 punitive damages against Pearson. The separate hearing officer’s postverdict judgment is outside this review; qualified immunity is not presented.
