## 1998-06-22 ? Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott; No. 97-581.

Source: pennsylvania_board_of_probation_parole_v_scott_merits_1998-06-22.md

### Event

Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott, No. 97-581. Argued March 30, 1998; decided June 22, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Pennsylvania Supreme Court. The question is whether the federal exclusionary rule applies when parole officers search their known supervisee unconstitutionally and the parole authority seeks to use the evidence in revocation.

Scott’s parole prohibited possession of weapons. Parole officers arrested him, obtained his residence keys and searched without obtaining consent; they found firearms, a bow and arrows. The Board admitted the evidence and recommitted him. The Commonwealth Court reversed, and the Pennsylvania Supreme Court affirmed that reversal on the federal exclusionary ground because the searchers knew his parole status. The Court assumes the Fourth Amendment violation for this remedial question and does not decide the validity or state-law basis of the lower court’s consent construction.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Complete bounded institutional-deterrence rule, reservations and affirmance. |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Joins the Court in full and adds a distinct constitutional-remedy explanation. |
| Dissent | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Would reverse the federal exclusion requirement and remand on the ordinary-revocation cost/deterrence ground. |

### Assignment statements

Form fixed first: one signed merits opinion decides the constitutional exclusionary remedy in this revocation proceeding, with a concurrence for Stevens’s additional constitutional-remedy ground and a dissent on the competing legal balance. It is counted after plenary argument. The majority rule is not split or narrowed for authorship; it preserves the actual institutional boundary and all supported exceptions.
Before-assignment fresh recount after Pearson: Stone-Zsela 12; Stevens 11; O'Connor 12; Scalia 11; Kennedy 12; Souter 12; Thomas 11; Ginsburg 12; Breyer 12. Last counted event: Pearson v. Hines, June 22, 1998. There are 105 counted opinions/credits. Eligible authors: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, each fully supporting the shared deterrence rule and remedy. Stevens’s additional constitutional basis does not make him ineligible. Minimum: 11. Pool: all five eligible Justices.

## 1998-06-22 ? United States v. Bajakajian; No. 96-1487.

Source: united_states_v_bajakajian_merits_1998-06-22.md

### Event

United States v. Bajakajian, No. 96-1487. Argued November 4, 1997; decided June 22, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit. The question is whether forfeiture of the entire $357,144 transported out of the country without the required report violates the Excessive Fines Clause.

Bajakajian pleaded guilty to willfully failing to report currency under 31 U.S.C. §§5316 and 5322. A separate false-statement count was dismissed by agreement, not decided by acquittal. After a forfeiture trial, the District Court found a lawful source, intended payment of a lawful debt and no connection to another crime. It refused full forfeiture under 18 U.S.C. §982(a)(1), ordered $15,000 forfeited and imposed three years’ probation and a $5,000 fine. Section 5322(a) authorized up to five years and $250,000; the applicable Guidelines maxima were six months and $5,000.

The Ninth Circuit affirmed on the ground that reporting currency was not an instrumentality and thus could not be forfeited, without reaching its proportionality prong. The Government seeks the full fund. Bajakajian did not cross-appeal the $15,000 forfeiture.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer throughout; Stone-Zsela in Parts I and III; O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy in Parts I and II | I: punitive coverage, gross disproportionality and rejection of a categorical instrumentality prerequisite. II: appellate review. III: actual excessiveness and bounded affirmance. |
| Dissent in part | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia | All three join Parts I–II but reject Part III’s application and would permit the full statutory forfeiture. |

### Assignment statements

Form fixed first: one signed plenary merits opinion decides punitive-fine coverage and the gross-disproportionality standard, the separate appellate-review proposition, and the application/remedy. The natural parts preserve actual join differences, including Stone’s lack of an authorized independent review-method ground; they are not created to change counts. One author can carry all parts, so the opinion is not divided among authors. It counts once.
Before-assignment fresh recount after Scott: Stone-Zsela 12; Stevens 11; O'Connor 12; Scalia 11; Kennedy 12; Souter 13; Thomas 11; Ginsburg 12; Breyer 12. Last counted event: Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott, June 22, 1998; 106 counted opinions/credits. Eligible authors for the complete opinion: Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. They support the affirmance and every proposition, including review. Stone supports the judgment and Parts I/III but cannot author the complete Part II rationale from silence; O’Connor, Scalia and Kennedy oppose the application and judgment. Minimum eligible count: 11. Pool: Stevens, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

Supp. 168. The questions concern the statutory review channel, each action's Article III standing and whether the challenged post-enactment cancellations violate Article I.

On August 11, 1997, the President canceled §4722(c) of the Balanced Budget Act and §968 of the Taxpayer Relief Act, each signed August 5. The former protected identified New York provider taxes from specified Medicaid recoupment consequences; the latter made a qualifying seller's tax deferral available for specified cooperative acquisitions. ## 1998-06-25 ? Eastern Enterprises v. Apfel, No. 97-42.

Source: eastern_enterprises_v_apfel_merits_1998-06-25.md

### Event

Eastern Enterprises v. Apfel, No. 97-42. Argued March 4, 1998; decided June 25, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the First Circuit, 110 F.3d 150, affirming summary judgment against Eastern's constitutional challenge to its Coal Act assessments. The questions are whether this retroactive tier-three liability violates the Takings or Due Process Clause and what relief follows. A renewed statutory-assignment claim is outside review.

Eastern employed the assigned retirees before 1966 and participated in the early benefit funds. Its direct mining ended in 1965, but subsidiary ownership and substantial dividends continued until 1987. Later benefit guarantees were added after its direct undertaking ended. Over 1,000 former employees were assigned under §9706(a)(3); annual premiums exceeded $5 million, while projected lifetime totals were disputed.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |

| Same concurrence, due-process section | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela | Independent ground for reversal and the same bounded relief | Exceptional remote, severe and disproportionate retrospective burden; two Justices |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm enforcement | No identified property taking and a rational retrospective allocation grounded in the actual workforce and benefit relationship |

No affirmative invalidity rationale commands five votes. Kennedy and the four dissenters directly agree on the negative Takings proposition stated below. Stone-Zsela does not join Kennedy's property section.

Form was fixed before counting: one signed opinion announcing the judgment on the three-Justice contextual takings ground; one Kennedy opinion concurring in the judgment, with a distinct property section and a due-process section; and one dissent stating the four affirmers' complete grounds. The lead's constitutional ground is supported by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas. All three can deliver it in full; Stone and Kennedy cannot because they do not join its takings or compensation-channel analysis. Stone participates in the reversing judgment and assigns the lead under §8. No multiplication of lead authors or artificial division changes the count. The independent five-Justice negative Takings proposition is identified across writings, without pretending those Justices agree on the judgment.

## 1998-06-25 ? Swidler & Berlin v. United States; No. 97-1192.

Source: swidler_berlin_v_united_states_merits_1998-06-25.md

### Event

Swidler & Berlin v. United States, No. 97-1192. Argued June 8, 1998; decided June 25, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the District of Columbia Circuit. The question is whether the attorney-client privilege survives death and whether later criminal-investigative importance permits a case-specific override.

As investigations concerning the dismissal of White House Travel Office employees began, Vincent Foster consulted attorney Hamilton for legal representation. Hamilton made handwritten notes. Foster died nine days later. A federal grand jury, at the Independent Counsel’s request, subpoenaed the notes in an investigation concerning possible crimes during the earlier investigations. The District Court denied enforcement on privilege grounds. The court of appeals adopted a posthumous exception for communications of substantial relative importance to particular criminal litigation.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Complete post-death survival rule, rejection of the asserted need balance, reservations and reversal. |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would permit a strictly confined inquiry into necessary information unavailable elsewhere and a compelling criminal interest; no final disclosure or work-product determination. |

### Assignment statements

Form fixed first: one signed merits opinion resolves the federal relationship privilege after plenary argument, with a dissent for the strictly limited post-death necessity exception. The work-product question is not added to obtain a larger disposition. This is a counted signed merits opinion; one common author can carry all majority propositions.
Before-assignment fresh recount: Stone-Zsela 12; Stevens 11; O'Connor 12; Scalia 11; Kennedy 12; Souter 13; Thomas 12; Ginsburg 12; Breyer 12. Last counted event: United States v. Bajakajian, June 22, 1998; 107 counted opinions/credits. Schacht’s intervening signed threshold opinion is excluded. Eligible authors: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, each supporting the full survival/no-general-balancing rationale and reversal. Minimum: 11. Pool: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; Souter’s 13 is outside the pool.
Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns to Stevens for fit. His Jaffee opinion directly explains why predictable protection cannot depend on ordinary later evidentiary need while keeping genuinely separate contours open. That work is the precise common-law privilege task here, with Upjohn supplying the distinct attorney-client relationship. Stone’s approved post-death confidentiality explanation is strong, but his count of 13 places him outside the pool; Stevens supplies the direct prior authoring fit and the lower count. Kennedy and the other pool members fully carry the ground but offer no greater case-specific advantage. No expansion is needed. O’Connor writes the minority’s strict necessity/unavailable-alternatives/compelling-interest account, which her reconciled position articulates while preserving the Jaffee distinction; Scalia and Thomas join. The majority opinion adds one credit for Stevens.

The questions concern the credited choir's use of religious music and venues, Free Exercise and compelled singing with the reported grade-neutral option, graduation's effect on prospective relief and a surviving damages controversy, and the denial of leave to amend the Establishment Clause claim as futile. The original federal complaint and reconsideration ruling are distinct from the proposed amendment. The District Court denied amendment as futile and alternatively untimely; the Court of Appeals did not decide untimeliness or immunity. Rachel Bauchman attended the elective, credit-bearing choir in 1994–95, later attended private school, and graduated in June 1997. Her mother sued only as parent and guardian. The reported option allowed her to decline objectionable songs without a grade reduction. The amendment alleged a continuing official devotional practice and supported it with earlier concrete incidents; those allegations remain unproved.

## 1998-06-26 ? Monge v. California, No. 97-6146.

Source: monge_v_california_merits_1998-06-26.md

### Event

Monge v. California, No. 97-6146. Argued April 28, 1998; decided June 26, 1998. October Term 1997, chunk 12. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of California, reviewing 16 Cal. 4th 826, 941 P.2d 1121 (1997). The question is whether the Double Jeopardy Clause bars another attempt to establish the serious-prior-felony allegation after the first proof failed to establish its necessary personal-conduct qualification.

The jury convicted Monge of using a minor to sell marijuana, selling or transporting marijuana, and possessing marijuana for sale. The information gave notice of a serious-prior-felony allegation and a distinct prior-prison-term allegation. Monge waived a jury on those allegations. At the bifurcated bench proceeding, the State introduced a prison record establishing an assault conviction and imprisonment, but not necessarily the required personal use of a dangerous or deadly weapon or personal infliction of great bodily injury. The prosecutor's assertion about use of a stick was not evidence of that missing fact. The trial court found both allegations true and imposed eleven years: a selected five-year term doubled to ten, plus one year for the separate prison-term allegation. A three-year term on count two was stayed and a two-year term on count three ran concurrently.

The State conceded on appeal that the first record did not prove the serious-prior qualification beyond a reasonable doubt. The Court of Appeal found insufficiency and barred retrial. The California Supreme Court reversed that double-jeopardy ruling, permitting another proof proceeding. The seven-year unenhanced maximum on count one, fourteen-year qualifying-prior maximum, selected five-year term and independent one-year enhancement remain distinct.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Breyer | Affirms renewed-proof permission | Parts I–II in full: this noncapital recidivist-status classification, nonattachment and bounded remedy |
| Dissent | Scalia | Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg | Would reverse renewed-proof permission | Constitutional element function of the additional personal-conduct qualification and resulting acquittal finality |
| Additional dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would reverse on an additional sufficient ground | Finality of this failed trial-like aggravating adjudication even if treated as genuine noncapital sentencing |

Stevens joins Scalia's case-confined element ground and separately gives a broader finality ground. His join adopts no general rule for every fact that increases a punishment maximum; the common dissent addresses this additional personal-conduct qualification and the completed failed adjudication. Scalia, Souter and Ginsburg do not join an extension of Bullington to genuine noncapital sentencing merely because trial safeguards were afforded. No combination of dissenting rationales creates a Court holding.

### Assignment statements

Form fixed before counting: one signed plenary Opinion of the Court on the narrow noncapital qualifying-status ground and its remedy; a Scalia dissent on the element-function ground with Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; and an additional Stevens dissent giving his distinct finality ground. No Marks aggregation or invented common extension of Bullington is used. The five affirmance supporters share the full Court rationale; the three element-ground Associates expressly reject Stevens's extension to genuine sentencing, while Stevens can join the confined element conclusion without abandoning his additional ground.

## 1998-07-20 ? Camilo-Robles v. Hoyos; First Circuit Nos. 97-2260, 97-2261, 97-2262 and 97-2264; 151 F.3d 1.

Camilo-Robles v. Hoyos, First Circuit Nos. 97-2260, 97-2261, 97-2262 and 97-2264; 151 F.3d 1. Plenary merits decision, July 20, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 12. On review of the First Circuit's June 29, 1998 judgment affirming four summary-judgment denials for five appellants. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is established.

The questions concern the reviewable legal immunity issues on the proper assumed facts, the psychiatrists' claimed Puerto Rico statutory defense to federal liability, their distinct federal contractor-eligibility question, and each appellant's qualified-immunity request. The lower court's affirmance, rather than a direct appeal of the District Court's order, is under review.

On the plaintiff-favorable account, Officer Diaz-Martinez arrested security guard Camilo-Robles after a parking dispute on May 13, 1994, handcuffed and shoved him into a cruiser, punched and slapped him while taking him to the station, and held him for about three hours before release without charges. The asserted personal responsibility of psychiatrists Hoyos and Rivera-Gonzalez and officials Diaz-Pagan, Santiago-Gonzalez and Toledo-Davila arises from their own evaluations, rearming, deployment or other alleged culpable contributions, not their titles. These are assumptions for legal review, not findings of constitutional wrongdoing.

The District Court denied the motions in a brief order before completion of the Rule 56(f) process and receipt of all oppositions. The First Circuit identified likely favorable assumptions from materials already in the trial record and excluded extra-record appellate material. Missing opposition was not a default or admission.

## 1998-08-03 ? Berryman v. Rieger; Sixth Circuit No. 96-2337; 150 F.3d 561.

Source: berryman_v_rieger_jurisdiction_and_sanctions_1998-08-03.md

### Event

Berryman v. Rieger, Sixth Circuit No. 96-2337; 150 F.3d 561. Jurisdictional and appellate-sanctions decision, August 3, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 12. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the Sixth Circuit's July 6, 1998 judgment dismissing an interlocutory appeal and imposing double costs and attorney fees. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is established.

The questions are whether the appeal as presented contains a separable legal immunity issue and whether the independent enhanced-cost and fee award rests on lawful authority and adequate process. The Court reviews the appellate judgment; it does not hear a new interlocutory factual appeal or decide the underlying constitutional claims.

Berryman's September 1992 complaint alleged denial of medical care after an injury and retaliation for his litigation against a nurse at another facility. His sworn account conflicted with the defendants' treatment records. In January 1995 the Sixth Circuit reversed summary judgment on that factual conflict. It did not decide qualified immunity, which had not been raised in the original District Court motion, and expressly permitted proper presentation on remand. After discovery, a later immunity motion was denied and defendants appealed. A preliminary Clerk's order declined dismissal at that time; after full briefing and argument the panel dismissed the appeal and imposed enhanced costs and fees under §1912. Amounts remained for submissions and any necessary amount-setting hearing; no dollar award is supplied.

O'Connor delivers the signed opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No constitutional merits or ultimate qualified-immunity ruling is made.

## 1998-08-17 ? Brewster v. Board of Education; Ninth Circuit No. 97-55203; 149 F.3d 971.

Source: brewster_v_board_of_education_merits_1998-08-17.md

### Event

Brewster v. Board of Education; Ninth Circuit No. 97-55203; 149 F.3d 971. Plenary merits decision on submission, August 17, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 12. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the Ninth Circuit's July 13, 1998 judgment awarding the individual officials personal damages immunity on the attendance-speech and salary-process claims. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is established.

The questions concern the particular legal speech-immunity ground, each defendant's individual position, and the independent salary-immunity grounds covering prospective reduction and reported retrospective recovery. The Court reviews those legal grounds on the proper assumptions; it does not determine disputed historical facts. The District Court's rejection of the distinct union-grievance claim, five state claims and failure-to-train claim is outside this review.

Brewster entered service as a probationary elementary teacher in September 1994. He reported attendance-accounting concerns affecting public funding, including errors to Principal Gary Furuno and a different fraud allegation to Superintendent Audrey Clarke. Furuno recommended nonrenewal on January 23, 1995. The Board met February 28, and a March notice ended service June 30. For this appeal the officials conceded attendance speech motivated Furuno and asserted no same-decision defense; those limits do not establish every fact at trial or another defendant's motive.

Assistant Superintendent Althea Jenkins initially placed Brewster at salary Step 15. Her December 2 and 7 memoranda identified deficient teaching-experience verification and a proposed Step 4 placement, invited response and preceded the December 13 Board action. Brewster and his representative met Jenkins December 15; she considered his administrative-experience argument and proposed comparator before confirming the reduction January 9, 1995. Initial entitlement and retrospective withholding are assumed. Exact withholding dates and amounts are not established.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–V | Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg throughout | Supports each Court disposition | Review, retained defense, bounded speech application, salary immunity and individual/procedural limits |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas | Support salary affirmance; dissent from both speech dispositions | Parts I, II, IV and V; not Part III's speech-protection, clarity or remedial application |
| Same opinion, limited joins | Breyer | Stone-Zsela | Agrees with review and other-individual speech vacatur; separately votes to vacate Furuno and both salary components | Part I; Part II only that contextual balancing supplies no automatic immunity; Part V only that failure of immunity establishes no constitutional violation, causation, damages, supervisory liability or right to trial |

### Assignment statements

Before counting or considering an author, the form is fixed as one signed plenary merits Opinion of the Court with component-specific direct majorities, plus the two separate writings needed to preserve genuine disagreements. It decides the merits of personal damages immunity, not merely access to review, and counts once under Engine §8. Related propositions belong in one lead because five Associates support every part; no artificial division or count-driven combination is made.
The fresh actual-table recount used `python -B tmp/chunk12_assignment_inventory.py`, reading current-term opinion tables rather than preceding numeric statements.
